= Apache Camel 4.x Upgrade Guide

This document is for helping you upgrade your Apache Camel application
from Camel 4.x to 4.y. For example, if you are upgrading Camel 4.0 to 4.2, then you should follow the guides
from both 4.0 to 4.1 and 4.1 to 4.2.

[NOTE]
====
https://github.com/apache/camel-upgrade-recipes/[The Camel Upgrade Recipes project] provides automated assistance for some common migration tasks.
Note that manual migration is still required.
See the xref:camel-upgrade-recipes-tool.adoc[documentation] page for details.
====

== Upgrading Camel 4.23 to 4.24

=== camel-opa - batch fail-open marker

Batch evaluation now sets `CamelOpaDecisionFailedOpen=true` when any element proceeds because its decision point
was unavailable and `failOpen=true`. Previously this marker was set only for single-element evaluation. Routes
that inspect the marker after a batch should account for it applying to any element; the per-element outcome
list identifies the affected elements. The existing `CamelOpaBatchDecision` Boolean list is unchanged.

=== camel-controlbus - the language command is off by default

The `language` command of the control bus (`controlbus:language:<name>`) evaluates the message body as an expression of the named language.
It is now off by default: a `controlbus:language` endpoint fails when it is created unless its language is listed in the new `allowedLanguages` option, on the component or on the endpoint:

[source,properties]
----
camel.component.controlbus.allowed-languages = simple
----

The `route` command (`controlbus:route?action=...`) is not affected.

=== camel-clickup - the webhook signature check no longer logs signatures

The webhook consumer still logs the body it validates at `DEBUG`, but no longer the signature from the `X-Signature` header, nor the signature it calculates.
The `InvalidMessageSignatureException` of a failed check no longer carries the calculated signature: `getCalculatedSignature()` returns `null` and is deprecated, as is the constructor that takes it.

=== camel-observability-services - promoted to Stable

The `camel-observability-services` component has been promoted from _Preview_ to _Stable_ support level.

=== camel-lra - promoted to Stable

The `camel-lra` component has been promoted from _Preview_ to _Stable_ support level.

=== KeyValueRepository and State Store - namespace partitioning on shared backends

In Camel 4.23, `state-store` endpoints stored keys directly into the underlying `KeyValueRepository` without namespacing. When a single `KeyValueRepository` was shared between multiple state stores, the Cache EIP, or repository adapters (such as `KeyValueAggregationRepository` or `KeyValueIdempotentRepository`), operations on one store (such as `clear()`) or key collisions could affect entries of another component or pattern.

Starting in 4.24:

* `StateStoreComponent` automatically namespaces keys on shared backend repositories (auto-discovered from the registry or referenced via `backend=#ref`) under `statestore:<storeName>:<key>`. Delimiter characters in store names are escaped to guarantee that store names sharing a prefix remain strictly isolated. Operations such as `clear()`, `keys()`, and `size()` are scoped to the store's namespace.
* Dedicated backends created by `StateStoreBackendFactory` or the default per-store in-memory fallback are not namespaced.
* To maintain backward compatibility with un-namespaced entries written by Camel 4.23 on a shared backend without migrating keys, users can opt out of namespacing by configuring `namespaced=false` on the endpoint (`state-store:myStore?namespaced=false`) or on the component (`camel.component.state-store.namespaced=false`).
* The Cache EIP stores keys directly without a namespace prefix, preserving documented cache invalidation recipes (`cache.delete(key)` and `cache.clear()`). Note that because cache keys are un-prefixed, calling `clear()` on a `KeyValueRepository` shared with the Cache EIP will clear all entries in that repository, including state-store entries. It is recommended to use a dedicated repository for the Cache EIP when co-located with state-store.
* `KeyValueAggregationRepository` safely verifies entry types before unmarshalling, ignoring unexpected types gracefully instead of failing with a class cast exception.

=== camel-core-model - doTry keeps doCatch and doFinally in its outputs

`doCatch` and `doFinally` written as properties of `doTry` in the YAML DSL are now kept in the outputs of the
`TryDefinition`, as the clauses of the Java and XML DSLs always were, after the steps whatever the order of the YAML
keys. Code that walks `getOutputs()` (route dumps, tooling) now sees them, and `getCatchClauses()`,
`getFinallyClause()` and `getOutputsWithoutCatches()` are derived from the outputs on each call: a clause removed from
the outputs, for example with advice-with, is no longer used, and changing the list returned by `getCatchClauses()`
no longer changes the doTry. Use `setCatchClauses` and `setFinallyClause`, or the outputs, instead.

=== camel-ehcache - shared CacheManager and changed defaults

The following user-visible changes take effect in 4.24:

* **Shared CacheManager.** Endpoints that have the same `cacheName` and no explicit `cacheManager`,
  `cacheManagerConfiguration`, or `configurationUri` now share one `CacheManager` per component instance.
  Previously each endpoint created its own unmanaged (`UserManagedCache`) cache.
  - The same name now means the same cache. `ehcache:foo?action=PUT` and `ehcache:foo?action=GET`
    now read and write the same entries.
  - All endpoints for a cache name must use compatible key and value types. Ehcache validates them
    exactly, so `ehcache:foo` and `ehcache:foo?valueType=java.lang.String` now fail at startup with
    a type-mismatch error.
  - The shared caches, and their entries, are closed when the last endpoint that uses the shared
    CacheManager stops (for example when the CamelContext stops).

* **Event delivery on default caches.** A consumer on a cache without an explicit configuration now
  receives events. Previously, the `UserManagedCache` fallback lacked event executor services, so
  listeners were silently never called.

* **Null body rejected.** PUT, PUT_IF_ABSENT and REPLACE without a body and without a
  `CamelEhcacheValue` header now fail with `CamelExchangeException` instead of silently storing the
  `Message` object.

* **User-supplied `cacheManager` caches persist.** Caches created on demand inside a user-supplied
  `cacheManager` (e.g. `ehcache:foo?cacheManager=#myManager`) are no longer removed when Camel stops;
  the lifecycle of the caches follows the lifecycle of the supplied manager.

=== camel-flowable - xml inbound channels parse the event with the Camel XML settings

The `flowable` producer now parses the event of an inbound channel of type `camel` with `deserializerType` set to
`xml` with the XML parser settings that Camel uses, which are also the settings of Flowable 8: external DTDs are not
loaded, external general and parameter entities are not included, entity references are kept as entity reference
nodes, and XInclude is not processed. Before, the Flowable 7 deserializer parsed the event with the JDK defaults.
An event that relies on an external DTD or an external entity no longer gets their content. A channel that sets
`deserializerDelegateExpression` keeps its own deserializer, which can parse such events.

=== camel-jpa - query parameters from CamelJpaParameters header are bound literally

In previous versions, string values supplied in the `CamelJpaParameters` header map were evaluated as Simple expressions by the JPA producer.

Starting in 4.24, parameter values provided via the `CamelJpaParameters` header are used as-is and bound literally as query parameters without expression evaluation. The endpoint URI option `parameters` continues to support Simple expression evaluation for route-configured parameters.

Routes that previously relied on evaluating Simple expressions within the `CamelJpaParameters` header should evaluate those expressions in the route before setting the header value.

=== camel-openfga - writeTuples and the read operations carry a relationship condition

`writeTuples` now honours a relationship condition set on a tuple in the message body: a `ClientTupleKey` built with
`.condition(..)`, or a `Map` whose `condition` entry is a map of a `name` and an optional `context`. Before, only the
`user`, `relation` and `object` of a tuple were written and any condition was dropped, so a conditioned grant was
stored unconditioned. A tuple with no condition is still written unconditioned, and `deleteTuples` still deletes by
identity and ignores a condition.

`readTuples` and `readChanges` now add a `condition` entry (a map of `name` and `context`) to each result map when the
stored tuple has one, so a condition can be read back and round-tripped into a write.

=== camel-mcp-server - error replies on /mcp are JSON-RPC error responses

With the embedded HTTP server (Camel Main and Camel JBang), a request on `/mcp` that fails before it reaches the MCP
session (wrong `Content-Type`, a message that cannot be parsed, a missing or unknown `Mcp-Session-Id`) is answered with
the same HTTP status as before, and a body that is a JSON-RPC error response without an `id`:
`{"jsonrpc":"2.0","error":{"code":-32600,"message":"Session not found"}}`. The messages are short and fixed. The error
code is `-32700` (parse error) for a message that cannot be parsed, and `-32600` (invalid request) otherwise; before,
a missing header or session id gave `-32601` and an unknown session `-32603`.

=== camel-ai-resource - fixed resourceUri cannot contain braces

Starting in Camel 4.24, `ai-resource` rejects a fixed `resourceUri` containing `{` or `}` at route startup. The MCP SDK interprets those characters as URI-template expressions. Routes that intend variable path segments must use `uriTemplate` instead, for example `+ai-resource:customer?uriTemplate=camel:///customers/{id}+`. If the braces are literal URI characters, percent-encode them as `%7B` and `%7D`.

=== camel-micrometer, camel-opentelemetry-metrics - tags and attributes from headers are used as given

The `micrometer` and `opentelemetry-metrics` producers now use the tags of the `CamelMetricsTags` header and the
attributes of the `CamelMetricsAttributes` header as given. Before, their keys and values were evaluated as Simple
expressions, in the same way as the `tags` and `attributes` URI options. The URI options are still evaluated per
exchange, and a tag or attribute from the header still overrides one from the URI with the same key.

A route that relied on a Simple expression in a header tag or attribute should use the `tags` / `attributes` URI
option instead (for example `tags.customer=${header.customerId}`), or compute the value in the route before
setting the header.

=== camel-elasticsearch-rest-client - the document id and the index name are each sent as one path segment

The producer now percent-encodes the document id and the index name, so each is sent as one path segment of the
request and Elasticsearch takes it literally. Before, they were placed into the path as given: a `/`, `?` or `#` in
the value changed the path of the request, and a space or a `%` made the request fail. A route that put extra path
segments or request parameters into the document id or the index name must change.

A document id of `.` or `..` is now rejected, as is an index name that is empty, `.` or `..`, or that contains `\`,
`/`, `?`, `"`, `<`, `>`, `|`, `#` or a space. A comma separated list of index names and patterns, such as
`logs-*,my_index`, still works. A `GET_BY_ID` response without the source of the document now gives a `null` body
instead of failing with a `NullPointerException`.

=== camel-ognl removed

The `camel-ognl` component has been removed. It was deprecated since Camel 4.14.

The `ognl()` builder method has been removed from the Java DSL (`ExpressionClause`, `LanguageBuilderFactory`,
`BeanFactoryDefinition`, and the mock builders). The `<ognl>` element has been removed from the XML DSL and
the `ognl:` prefix from the YAML DSL.

Users should migrate to other expression languages:

* For OGNL-style property navigation, use the xref:components:languages:simple-ognl.adoc[Simple language]
  which supports OGNL-style notation (e.g. `${body.address.street}`).
* For full scripting, use xref:components:languages:groovy-language.adoc[Groovy] or
  xref:components:languages:mvel-language.adoc[MVEL].

=== camel-a2a - consumer context is separate from producer headers

An A2A consumer no longer populates producer control headers with the incoming operation and
identity. Update consumer routes that read these headers:

[cols="1,1",options="header"]
|===
| Previous consumer header | Replacement consumer header / exchange property
| `CamelA2AOperation` | `CamelA2ARequestOperation`
| `CamelA2ATaskId` | `CamelA2ARequestTaskId`
| `CamelA2AContextId` | `CamelA2ARequestContextId`
|===

To control a consumer's `SendMessage` reply, replace writes to `CamelA2ATaskState` with
`CamelA2AReplyTaskState`, and writes to `CamelA2AResponseType` with `CamelA2AReplyType`.
The old names continue to control producers or describe their responses. Explicit producer
operation headers still override the endpoint's `operation` option.

Java code using `A2AConstants` must also migrate even though the existing constant names and values
remain. For consumer metadata reads, replace `OPERATION`, `TASK_ID` and `CONTEXT_ID` with
`REQUEST_OPERATION`, `REQUEST_TASK_ID` and `REQUEST_CONTEXT_ID`, respectively. For consumer reply
controls, replace `TASK_STATE` and `RESPONSE_TYPE` with `REPLY_TASK_STATE` and `REPLY_TYPE`.

No Endpoint DSL header helper methods were removed. The new `a2ARequestOperation()`,
`a2ARequestTaskId()` and `a2ARequestContextId()` methods provide the consumer metadata header names;
`a2AReplyTaskState()` and `a2AReplyType()` provide the consumer reply control header names.
Existing helpers retain their producer roles.

Downstream results no longer replace the outer task ID, context, state or reply type, and progress
updates remain attached to the outer task. Consumer reply messages use the outer context and task
identity. With `dataFormat=POJO`, forwarding the same incoming `Message` instance to another agent
no longer carries the outer task/context IDs. A new message supplied by the route retains its IDs,
including an equal copy made with `Message.builder(incoming).build()`. When copying or transforming
the incoming message to start a new downstream conversation, clear inherited IDs with
`.taskId(null).contextId(null)`. Set explicit downstream IDs when continuing a downstream conversation.

=== camel-shiro - ShiroSecurityPolicy scopes SecurityManager to policy instance

The `ShiroSecurityPolicy` constructors that load configuration from an INI file or object no longer set the resulting
`SecurityManager` as Shiro's global security manager via `SecurityUtils.setSecurityManager(...)`. `ShiroSecurityProcessor`
now creates and authenticates the caller identity (principal) using the route policy's `SecurityManager` directly.
Applications configuring multiple `ShiroSecurityPolicy` instances in the same JVM now have their realms evaluated
independently per policy.

Code that accesses the ambient security context or calls `SecurityUtils.getSecurityManager()` outside the policy and
previously relied on the policy constructor setting the global security manager will now receive
`UnavailableSecurityManagerException`. Such applications should explicitly configure
`SecurityUtils.setSecurityManager(policy.getSecurityManager())` at startup.

Additionally:
- A `ShiroSecurityPolicy` created with the no-arg constructor must be configured with `setSecurityManager(...)`.
- When `alwaysReauthenticate=true` (the default), no caller identity stays bound to the processing thread after the
  policy check completes.

=== camel-ai-tool - required arguments with reserved names reject the call

Required tool arguments whose names start with `camel` or `org.apache.camel.` (case-insensitive)
now reject the invocation before the tool route runs. Previously, these arguments could pass the
required-argument check and then be discarded by the reserved-name filter, leaving the route without
a required value. Rename these parameters and update their `parameter.*` or `argSchema` declarations.

=== camel-azure-functions - invokeFunction checks the function app and the function name

The `invokeFunction` operation now checks the function app and the function name, from the endpoint or from the
`CamelAzureFunctionsFunctionApp` and `CamelAzureFunctionsFunctionName` headers, before it calls the function, and
builds the URL with the function name encoded as a path. The exchange fails with an `IllegalArgumentException` when the
function app is missing or is not an app name (letters, digits and hyphens, not starting or ending with a hyphen, at
most 60 characters), or when the function name has a `?`, `#`, `\` or `%`, an empty segment, or a `.` or `..`
segment. A function name of several segments, such as `products/42` for a function with a custom route, is still
accepted. The `x-functions-key` header is only added to an `https` URL on an `azurewebsites.net` host. Valid
configurations are not affected.

=== camel-mcp-server - the Origin header is checked on /mcp

On Camel Main and Camel JBang, the streamable HTTP MCP endpoint now checks the `Origin` header on every request
(`POST`, the SSE `GET` and `DELETE`), as the MCP streamable HTTP transport requires. A request without an `Origin`
header is served as before; MCP clients that run as a process send none. A request with an `Origin` header is served
only when the origin is allowed, and gets HTTP 403 otherwise.

By default only loopback origins are allowed (`localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]`, on any port). A browser-based
client served from another origin must now be listed in the new `camel.server.mcp-allowed-origins` option, for example
`+camel.server.mcp-allowed-origins = https://app.example.com, http://localhost:*+`. The list replaces the loopback
default. Scheme and host are compared ignoring case and a default port may be left out; `+*+` and other entries that are
not of the form `scheme://host[:port]` fail the startup. Stdio mode is not affected, and neither are the Spring Boot and
Quarkus engines.


=== camel-oauth - the scp claim is included in scope checks

JWT validation and token introspection now read scopes from both the standard `scope` claim and the
`scp` claim used by some identity providers. String values are split on whitespace; array values
are read as individual scopes. When both claims are present their values are combined and duplicate
scopes are removed. Consequently, a token that has a required scope only in `scp` can now pass an
`OAuthTokenValidationResult.hasScope(...)` check where it did not pass before. Review authorization
rules that intentionally relied on ignoring `scp`.

=== camel-mina-sftp - checking whether a file exists takes one lstat request

The `mina-sftp` component now checks whether a remote file exists (`fileExist=Ignore|Fail|Move`, `tempFileName`,
`tempPrefix`, a producer `doneFileName`) with an `lstat` request instead of `stat`. `readLock=changed` reads the size
and modification time of the file with the same request, instead of a listing of the directory. Each check is one
request, whatever the number of files in the directory, and `fastExistsCheck` no longer makes a difference for
`readLock=changed`: with `fastExistsCheck=true`, the read lock is now acquired, where before the file was never
consumed. The answer can differ from before in two cases:

* A dangling symbolic link now exists: the link itself is checked, not its target. For example, with
  `fileExist=Fail`, sending a file with the name of a dangling link now fails, where before the file was written.
* When the server answers the request with another status than "no such file", for example because it does not permit
  it on the path, the component lists the directory and compares the names, where before the file counted as missing.
  When the server does not answer that listing either, or the connection fails, the check now fails with an error.

`readLock=changed` keeps comparing the size and modification time of a symbolic link itself, as the directory listing
reported them. See the xref:components::mina-sftp-component.adoc[MINA SFTP component].

=== camel-mina-sftp - TCP_NODELAY is set on the connections

The `mina-sftp` component now sets `TCP_NODELAY` on its connections, as the JSch-based `sftp` component does.
Apache MINA SSHD leaves it off by default, so a small request, such as one that reads the next part of a directory
listing, could wait for the server to acknowledge the previous reply. The `org.apache.sshd.config.tcp-nodelay` system
property no longer applies to the connections of `mina-sftp`.

=== camel-master - the consumer keeps the leadership until its delegated consumer has stopped

When a `master:` consumer stops (its route stops, or the CamelContext shuts down), it now stops the delegated consumer
first, then the processor of the route, and only then releases the cluster view, so the leadership moves to another node
once this node no longer consumes. Before, it released the view first, so another node could start consuming while this
one was still stopping its consumer (a consumer may wait in its stop for the messages it is processing, as JMS does, and
these messages now reach a started route). The hand-over on a graceful stop can therefore come later, by up to the time
the delegated consumer takes to stop (a stop that blocks holds back the failover until it returns). Exchanges in flight
that the delegated consumer does not wait for in its stop may still be in flight when the leadership moves, as before.

=== camel-ftp - sftp checks whether a file exists with one lstat request

The `sftp` component now checks whether a remote file exists (`fileExist=Ignore|Fail|Move`, `tempFileName`,
`tempPrefix`, a producer `doneFileName`), and reads the size and modification time of the file for `readLock=changed`,
with a single `lstat` request instead of a listing of the directory. Each check is one request, whatever the number of
files in the directory, and `fastExistsCheck` no longer makes a difference for these checks. The answer can differ from
before in two cases:

* A dangling symbolic link now exists also with `fastExistsCheck=true`, as it already did without that option: the
  link itself is checked, not its target.
* On a server with a case-insensitive file system, such as one backed by Windows or macOS, a file whose name differs
  only in case now exists: before, the names of the listing were compared case-sensitively. For example, with
  `fileExist=Fail`, sending `REPORT.TXT` to a directory that holds `report.txt` now fails, where before it replaced
  `report.txt`.

When the server answers `lstat` with another status than "no such file", for example because it does not permit or
support it on the path, the component lists the directory as before. `readLock=changed` keeps comparing the size and
modification time of a symbolic link itself, as the directory listing reported them. See the
xref:components::sftp-component.adoc[SFTP component].

=== camel-ftp - sftp uses remote file and directory names as given

The `sftp` component now uses a remote file or directory name exactly as given, also when it contains `*` or `?`.
Before, the name was passed on to the JSch library, which reads `*` and `?` as wildcards, so storing, retrieving,
deleting, renaming, listing or changing to a directory could resolve the name to another file or directory whose name
matched, or fail when more than one name matched. With the default `separator=UNIX`, a `\` in a name is a path
separator, as before.

If you relied on a wildcard to select a file or a directory:

* A consumer with `useList=false` and a wildcard in `fileName`: list the directory instead, and select the files with
the `include` or `antInclude` option.
* A producer with a wildcard in the `CamelFileName` header or the `fileName` option: use the real name of the file.
* A wildcard in the directory of the endpoint: use the real name of the directory.

See the xref:components::sftp-component.adoc[SFTP component].

=== camel-kafka - exactlyOnce: a rejected send that a doCatch catches skips the record

With `exactlyOnce`, a Kafka send through the source producer after an earlier send failed in the same transaction is
rejected and marks the exchange rollback-only (a redelivery of the failed send, or another send such as in a `loop`).
When a `doCatch` catches that rejected send in the route of the send, the failure now counts as handled, as when an
error handler handles it: the output transaction is aborted and the source offsets are committed alone, so the record
is skipped. Before, the source transaction was rolled back and the record replayed. A `doCatch` of a route that called
the route of the send (for example with `direct`) behaves the same way, as it did in 4.23. To replay the record, throw
the exception again in the `doCatch` block, or end it with `rollback()`.

=== camel-jms, camel-sjms, camel-sjms2 - an exception sent back with transferException is not rolled back

With `transferException=true`, a consumer that sends the exception of a failed exchange back as the reply now always
treats the message as processed. Before, when the exception was a `RuntimeCamelException`, or had one as a cause, the
consumer also rethrew it: a transacted session was rolled back, so the request was redelivered and the reply was lost
with it. Now the outcome does not depend on the type of the exception. To have a failed request redelivered, do not
use `transferException` on that route.

=== camel-sjms, camel-sjms2 - a received StreamMessage is an InputStream

The camel-sjms and camel-sjms2 consumers now map a received `jakarta.jms.StreamMessage` to an `InputStream` over its
bytes, as the camel-jms consumer does, so a body sent with `jmsMessageType=Stream` arrives as the bytes that were sent.
Before, the body was the `jakarta.jms.StreamMessage` itself. To get the JMS message, use `mapJmsMessage=false`.

=== camel-keycloak - Keycloak 26.8.2 requires the kc-admin-api client scope

This is a change in Keycloak, not in Camel: from Keycloak 26.8.2 the Admin REST API accepts only access tokens that
carry the built-in `kc-admin-api` client scope, and rejects other tokens with `401 Unauthorized`. The Keycloak upgrade
assigns the scope to `admin-cli` and `security-admin-console` only. When the `keycloak` producer authenticates with
`clientId` and `clientSecret` of your own client, assign `kc-admin-api` to that client as a default client scope; a
pre-obtained `accessToken` must be issued with that scope too. Username and password authentication uses `admin-cli`
and keeps working. The integration tests of camel-keycloak now run against Keycloak 26.8.2.

=== Java

*Breaking change:* Camel 4.24 requires *Java 21* as the minimum runtime version.
Java 17 is no longer supported. Upgrade your JDK to 21 or later before upgrading to Camel 4.24.

=== camel-core - Package scanning of JARs no longer includes sibling packages

When scanning classes in a package (for example `camel.main.base-package-scan=com.acme.routes`, or
`PackageScanClassResolver.findImplementations`), classes found in a JAR were also included when they belonged to a
sibling package whose name merely started with the scanned package name, such as `com.acme.routesupport` or
`com.acme.routes2`. Scanning a directory on the classpath never did this. Both now only include the scanned package
and its sub packages, so a `RoutesBuilder` or `@Configuration` class in such a sibling package is no longer
discovered when running from a JAR; list that package explicitly if it should be scanned.

=== camel-core - Model has a new getModelLock method

The `org.apache.camel.model.Model` interface (and so `ModelCamelContext`) has a new method `getModelLock()`, which
returns the lock held while routes are added to or removed from the model. A custom implementation of `Model` must
implement it (classes that extend `DefaultCamelContext` need no change). The Kamelet component now creates the routes of
Kamelets holding this lock.

=== camel-core - CamelContext has a new removeRouteTemplatesFromResources method

The `CamelContext` interface has a new method `removeRouteTemplatesFromResources(String pattern)`, which removes the
route templates loaded from a resource (such as a route file or a Kamelet) and keeps those without one (such as the route
templates of a Java `RouteBuilder` added to the context). A custom implementation of `CamelContext` must implement it
(classes that extend `DefaultCamelContext` need no change). The route reload of dev mode uses it, so a route template
added from Java is kept across a reload, and a route created from it with a templated route in a file is created again.

=== camel-core - Route.clearRouteModel() is deprecated

The `Route.clearRouteModel()` method is deprecated and is now a no-op. It will be removed in a later release.

This method dropped the references a runtime route held to its model (the route definition, the error handler factory
and the error handlers) once the route was created at startup. The `CamelContext` already keeps the route definitions
in its model, so clearing them on the route saved little memory at the cost of making `Route.getRoute()` and
`Route.getErrorHandlerFactory()` return `null` for routes created at startup (but not for routes added after startup).

Applications that called `Route.clearRouteModel()` directly can remove the call. Implementations of the `Route` interface
that override `clearRouteModel()` can remove the override.

=== camel-core - the route topology says whether a route waits for the answer, and has the edges of a wire tap

Each edge of the route topology (the `route-topology` developer console, the topology JSON of the dump routes strategy
and `RouteTopologyDumper`) now says whether the route waits for the answer of the route it sends to: `reply` is `true`
for a send to `direct`, and for a send that is InOut, as a request-reply over a JMS queue is
(`exchangePattern=InOut`). The JSON of the console and the topology JSON write `reply` on every edge, and the text
output of the console marks a request-reply with `request-reply`. The `RouteTopologyDumper.TopologyEdge` record has the
new `reply` component; its seven-argument constructor is kept and creates an edge without it.

A `wireTap` or a `toD` to an endpoint the route names, rather than one it computes with an expression, is now an edge
too (a wire tap never waits for the answer), and an external endpoint when no route consumes from it.

=== camel-console - the processor-detail console names the route input by the id of its from

The first entry of the `processor-detail` developer console, the route input (`type` is `from`), now has the id of the
`from` of the route as `id` (such as `from1`, or the id given to the `from`), which the tracer, the message history and
the backlog debugger name it by. It had the route id before, which is still the `routeId` of the response.

=== camel-management - LastExchangeCompletedExchangeId is updated at most once per millisecond

The JMX attribute `LastExchangeCompletedExchangeId`, of the `CamelContext`, route and processor MBeans, is now updated
at most once per millisecond, so that the JMX statistics no longer generate an exchange id for every exchange. When
several exchanges complete within the same millisecond, the attribute has the id of the first one, not of the last one.
Failed exchanges are not affected: `LastExchangeFailureExchangeId` is still updated for every failed exchange.

=== camel-core - the activity of the backlog tracer is replaced by the timeline dev console

The activity tracking of the backlog tracer (since 4.22) is removed. It kept the last 100 completed exchanges, which
under load was only the last seconds. The new `timeline` dev console takes its place: for each second of the last 5
minutes it records what each route did (completed, recovered by a redelivery, handled by the error handler, failed),
the mean, p95 and max time, the errors by route and exception, and per second the failed, handled and recovered exchanges and
the 3 slowest with the remote calls they made. It is on whenever the dev consoles are, as with `camel run` (profile `dev`), and the
number of seconds is set with `camel.devConsole.timeline.seconds` (60 to 3600, default 300).

Removed:

* `BacklogTracer.isActivityEnabled`, `setActivityEnabled`, `getActivitySize`, `setActivitySize`, `getActivity`,
  `dumpActivity` and `dumpActivityAsJSon`, and the interface `org.apache.camel.spi.BacklogTracerActivityMessage`.
* The JMX attributes `ActivityEnabled` and `ActivitySize`, and the operation `dumpActivityAsJSon`, of the backlog
  tracer MBean.
* The options `camel.trace.activityEnabled` and `camel.trace.activitySize` (also of Spring Boot and Quarkus).
* The exchange property `Exchange.ACTIVITY_SPAN_TAGS` (`CamelActivitySpanTags`), which camel-telemetry set when
  activity tracking was on.
* The `activity` dev console, which the `timeline` dev console replaces.
* `camel get activity`, which `camel get timeline --exchanges` replaces. `camel get timeline` shows a line per route,
  and `--errors` the errors by route and exception.
* The `<pid>-activity.json` file and the `activity` snapshot of the CLI connector, which are now `<pid>-timeline.json`
  (the last 10 seconds) and `timeline`. The new action `timeline` returns the whole timeline.

=== camel-management - an exchange recovered by a redelivery is counted when the route removes the Camel headers

The `Redeliveries` statistics of the routes, processors and the `CamelContext` count an exchange that completed after a
redelivery also when the route removed the `CamelRedelivered` header afterwards (such as `removeHeaders("Camel*")`
before calling an endpoint). Before, such a recovery was not counted. `ExchangeHelper.isRedelivered` now also returns
`true` when the error handler redelivered the exchange, without looking at the header only. An exchange whose failure
is continued (`continued(true)`) no longer keeps its redelivery counter, as with a handled failure.

=== camel-management - the input and reply hits of the runtime endpoint registry count each message once

The runtime endpoint registry (in extended statistics mode, as the `RuntimeEndpointRegistry` MBean, the endpoints dev
console and the Camel CLI show it) now counts an input hit of a route when an exchange enters the route through its
consumer, as the route counts its exchanges. Before, it counted every created exchange: the copy of a Circuit Breaker
and the sub-exchanges of a Split, Multicast or Recipient List were counted as input hits of the route as well, and a
route that consumes from `direct` (or got a message sent by a `ProducerTemplate` to its `direct` endpoint) had no input
hits at all.

Likewise, the reply of an InOut consumer is counted as an out hit of its endpoint when the exchange is done in the route,
once per message. Before, the completed copy of a Circuit Breaker and the sub-exchanges of a Split, Multicast or
Recipient List were counted as replies as well.

=== camel-core - a step after a redelivered step gets all its redeliveries

When a step of a route was redelivered and then succeeded, a later failure (of another step of the route, or of a
route that gets the exchange or a copy of it next, such as over `seda`) counted its redeliveries on from the
`CamelRedeliveryCounter` header that the first step left on the message. With `maximumRedeliveries(2)`, a step after one
that needed two redeliveries was not redelivered at all: the message went to the dead letter channel at its first
failure. Now such a failure is redelivered as any other, from attempt 1, so such routes can redeliver more often than
before. The `CamelRedelivered`, `CamelRedeliveryCounter` and `CamelRedeliveryMaxCounter` headers stay on the message
after the redelivered step succeeded, as before.

The same applies after a `doCatch` caught the failure of a route that the `doTry` called (such as with `direct`) and
whose error handler redelivered it: a later failure (after the `doTry`, in a route the `doCatch` or `doFinally` block
calls, in a calling route when the `doCatch` block throws, or in a route called later) is redelivered from attempt 1.
The redelivery headers of the called route stay readable in the `doCatch` block.

=== camel-core - a nested split no longer changes the processed items of the outer split

With `errorThreshold` or `maxFailedRecords` on a split that contains another split, the inner split changed the number of
processed items of the outer split. It counted its own items when it had no thresholds, and hid the items of the outer
split when it had them. `SplitResult.getProcessedItems()` now reports the items of that split only. A nested split with
`errorThreshold` can now stop earlier than before, as the ratio is computed from the correct number.

=== camel-util - BoundedExecutorService: a task of the executor does not wait for a permit

With virtual threads, a task that runs in a thread pool bounded by `maxQueueSize` and submits more work to it, such as a
parallel split, no longer waits for a permit when none is available. It runs the work itself at once, whatever the
rejected policy. Before, it could wait for a permit it held itself, until `keepAliveTime` expired, or forever with
`Block`. A split with a `timeout` could lose items that way. With `Abort`, a parallel split ran one item fewer at a time
than `maxQueueSize` allows, and with `maxQueueSize` 1 it was rejected after `keepAliveTime`. A submitter outside the
pool waits, and is rejected with `Abort`, as before.

=== camel-core - suspendRoute on a route that is not started leaves it as it is

`suspendRoute` on a route that is not started (stopped, or with `autoStartup=false` and never started), from the
`RouteController`, JMX, the control bus, the CLI (`camel cmd suspend-route`) or a route policy such as
`DurationRoutePolicy`, now leaves the route in its current state, and route policies no longer get `onSuspend` for it.
Before, the route became `Suspended`, and a later `startRoute` or `resumeRoute` resumed it without starting its
consumer, so it consumed nothing. Code that checks the status of a route after `suspendRoute` now sees `Stopped` for
such a route. The last error of such a route, for example from a failed start, is kept.

=== camel-core - Routing Slip and Dynamic Router fire the sent event of a step before the next step

The `ExchangeSentEvent` of a step of a `routingSlip` or `dynamicRouter` is now fired when that step is done, before the
next step is sent, as with `recipientList`. Before, when a step completed asynchronously (nearly every step, as the
error handler of each step schedules its work), the sent events of the steps were fired in reverse order after the last
step: the time taken of a step included all the steps after it, and the sent event of the first step carried the
message and the failure after the last step.

Users of the sent events see the change: the time of a step is lower (such as in the exchange event notifier timers of
`camel-micrometer` and `camel-opentelemetry-metrics`), the first step is no longer reported as failed because a later
step failed, and event-based tracing (`camel-jfr`, and `camel-telemetry` together with its own fix for asynchronous
steps) pairs the events of each step. There is no option to keep the old order.

=== camel-core - RedeliveryErrorHandler.defensiveCopyExchangeIfNeeded is deprecated

When redelivery is enabled, the error handler (`DefaultErrorHandler`, `DeadLetterChannel` and the transaction error
handlers) keeps a copy of the message before each step of the route, so a redelivery starts again from the original
message. It copied the whole exchange for that, at every step, also when the step succeeded, but a redelivery only ever
restored the IN message from it. It now keeps a copy of the IN message only. Routes behave as before: exchange
properties and variables were not restored on redelivery, and still are not.

The protected `RedeliveryErrorHandler.defensiveCopyExchangeIfNeeded(Exchange)` method is deprecated and no longer called.
A custom error handler that overrides it to change what is redelivered must change the message before the redelivery
instead, for example with `onRedelivery`.

=== camel-core - the post-quantum TLS named groups: the NIST hybrids count, and an order that already leads with a hybrid is kept

`SSLContextParameters` prefers a hybrid post-quantum key exchange on a JVM that offers one, when no named groups and no
named groups filter are configured (see the
xref:camel-configuration-utilities.adoc#_auto_configuration_zero_config_pqc[auto-configuration of the JSSE utility]).
Three things changed:

* Only `X25519MLKEM768` was recognized. `SecP256r1MLKEM768` and `SecP384r1MLKEM1024` now count too, so a JVM or a crypto
  policy restricted to the NIST curves, which enables only those two, gets the post-quantum preference it did not get
  before.
* An order that already leads with a hybrid group is kept as it is. It was rewritten to `X25519MLKEM768, x25519,
  secp256r1, secp384r1, ...` before. An operator who put another hybrid first through `jdk.tls.namedGroups`, for example
  a CNSA 2.0 setup with `SecP384r1MLKEM1024` first, keeps that order, and the default order of JDK 27 (JEP 527), which
  leads with `X25519MLKEM768`, is no longer rewritten.
* The `INFO` line `Auto-configured PQC named groups: [...]` is therefore logged only when Camel reorders the groups. On a
  JVM whose order already leads with a hybrid, and on one that enables no hybrid at all, a `DEBUG` line of
  `org.apache.camel.support.jsse.SSLContextParameters` says why nothing was done. Anything that relied on the `INFO` line
  as the confirmation that a post-quantum key exchange is preferred should read the named groups of a created
  `SSLEngine` or `SSLSocket` instead: `getSSLParameters().getNamedGroups()`.

=== camel-core - the documented default of skipBindingOnErrorCode is true

The `skipBindingOnErrorCode` option of the Rest DSL (on `restConfiguration`, `rest`, its verbs and `restBinding`) is now
documented with the default value `true`, the default it has always had at runtime. Before, the model, the catalog, the
XML and YAML DSL schemas said `false`. Routes behave as before.

Tools that read the default from the catalog or the schemas now see `true`.

=== camel-core - resolveResource: the name of the resource has no scheme

With `resolveResource=true` on an expression (new in 4.23), a result that starts with `resource:` must now name a
resource without a scheme, which is loaded from the classpath. A result with a scheme (`resource:file:order.json`,
`resource:classpath:...`, `resource:http:...`, `resource:ref:...`) or with `..` in the name fails with an
`IllegalArgumentException` that says to give the name without a scheme. Before, any scheme was loaded, from any
location.

Drop the scheme: return `resource:order.json` instead of `resource:file:order.json`. The same name works with
`camel run`, which puts the files given on the command line on the classpath, and after `camel export`, which puts them
in `src/main/resources`.

[source,yaml]
----
- setBody:
    expression:
      groovy:
        expression: "counter == 1 ? 'resource:order.json' : 'resource:order-bad-email.json'"
        resolveResource: true
----

Let the route choose the name, such as a fixed folder with a name the route picks, rather than taking it straight from
the message. The `resource:` prefix on the expression text itself, such as `constant: "resource:file:order.json"`, is
not affected.

=== camel-core - pollEnrich: the body is null after a timeout also for a message that creates its body on demand

When `pollEnrich` (or `poll`) times out and no `aggregationStrategy` is configured, the body is set to `null`, and with
`variableReceive` the variable is `null`. For a message that creates its body on demand, the `null` did not stick: the
next read created the body again. The message of a file consumer creates its body from its file, so after a timeout
the body, or the variable of `variableReceive`, was the file of the incoming exchange instead of `null`. The messages
of the JMS, sjms, mail, HTTP, XMPP, SMPP, SNMP and Spring WS consumers create their body on demand as well, so there it
was the payload of the consumer again.

The body, or the variable, is now `null` after a timeout for these consumers too. A route that read the incoming file
or payload from the body after a timed out `pollEnrich` must keep it before the `pollEnrich` instead, for example with
`variableReceive`, which leaves the body as it was. To keep the body empty, the message is replaced by a plain message
with the same message id, headers and attachments, so after a timeout it is no longer of the message type of the
consumer (such as `GenericFileMessage` or `JmsMessage`).

=== camel-core - Loop EIP breakOnShutdown

The Loop EIP with `breakOnShutdown` now only breaks out when the route (or Camel) is stopped, and no longer on suspend.
A suspend waits for the loop like any other inflight exchange. A loop that never ends on its own (such as a
`doWhile` with `breakOnShutdown`) is therefore no longer broken at once by a suspend: the suspend waits until the
shutdown timeout, and the forced suspend then breaks the loop and stops the consumer of the route.

=== camel-core - doFinally runs all its steps after a called route handled a failure

When a `doTry` calls a route (such as with `direct`) whose error handler handles a failure (a dead letter channel or a
handled `onException`), the `doFinally` block now runs all its steps. Before, in all Camel 4.x releases, it ran only its
first step and skipped the rest, so a second or later step of the block (such as a `to`, a recipient list or a routing
slip) did not send its message. A route called from such a `doFinally` that fails is now handled (or continued) by its
own error handler, as anywhere else; before, its error handler left the failure alone and the exchange failed with that
exception. As before, the route does not continue after the `doTry` once a called route handled the failure.

=== camel-core - an AsyncProcessor that throws on the first attempt goes through the error handler

When the error handler of a step can redeliver, has an `onException` or an `onPrepareFailure` processor, or is a dead
letter channel, and the processor of the step (such as a custom `AsyncProcessor` used with `process(...)`; an endpoint
in `to(...)` is not affected) throws from `process(exchange, callback)` on the first attempt instead of setting the
exception on the exchange, the error handler now handles that failure as any other failure of the step: it redelivers
it, and when the redeliveries are exhausted, the matching `onException` or the dead letter channel handles it. This is
what it already did when the processor set the same exception on the exchange, or when a plain `Processor` threw it.
Before, the exchange failed at once with the thrown exception. When such a processor calls its callback and then
throws, the exception is now only logged at WARN; when it handed its callback to another thread before it threw, and
that thread calls it after the error handler took over the failure, or when it calls its callback twice, that call is
now ignored and logged at WARN. Before, the exchange was continued a second time in these cases.

To not redeliver such a failure, add an `onException` for its exception type with `maximumRedeliveries(0)`; as for the
same failure set on the exchange, the dead letter channel and the `onException` still handle it. There is no option to
let such a failure skip the dead letter channel or `onException` as before; a plain `Processor` that throws gets the
same handling. Better, fix the processor to set the exception on the exchange and call the callback, as an
`AsyncProcessor` must.

=== camel-core - Aggregate EIP no longer completes the groups when the route is suspended

With `forceCompletionOnStop` or `completeAllOnStop`, suspending the route no longer completes the aggregated groups.
They are kept and completed when the route or Camel is stopped. To send them out before a suspend, use
`forceCompletionOfAllGroups` (`AggregateController` or JMX), or stop the route instead.

=== camel-core - Aggregate EIP does not confirm a group that was completed by its first exchange

A group that is completed by the exchange that creates it, for example with `completionSize(1)`, is never added to the
aggregation repository. With a `RecoverableAggregationRepository`, Camel no longer calls `confirmWithResult` for such a
group. Before, `JdbcAggregationRepository` logged an ERROR for each one and kept its exchange id as unconfirmed until
the route was removed. A custom `RecoverableAggregationRepository` should not expect `confirmWithResult` for these
groups.

=== camel-core - ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy counts the failures within the failure window

The circuit of `ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy` now opens when `failureThreshold` failures occurred within the last
`failureWindow` milliseconds, as documented. Before, it counted every failure since the circuit last closed (or since
the route started), and only the last failure had to be within the window, so failures spread over hours opened the
circuit on a single new failure. Routes whose failures are spread out therefore open their circuit less often now; to
open it sooner, lower `failureThreshold` or raise `failureWindow`.

In the half open state, a successful exchange now closes the circuit, and a failed exchange opens it again. Before, a
successful exchange opened the circuit again as long as the failures that opened it were within `failureWindow`; with
the defaults (a window of 60 seconds, a half open check after 30 seconds) a success in the first 30 seconds after the
half open check therefore kept the circuit open. A `halfOpenHandler` decides as before.

=== camel-core - the failover load balancer does not fail over on a suppressed exception

The failover load balancer with a list of exceptions now matches only the exception the attempt fails with and its
causes, as `onException` and `doCatch` do. Before, it also matched the suppressed exceptions of that exception. Since
4.23, an exception thrown in a `doFinally` block is added as a suppressed exception to the exception from the `doTry`
block, so a `doFinally` that threw one of the exceptions to fail over on made the load balancer fail over, and the
exception from the `doTry` block was lost when the next processor succeeded. Now the exchange fails with that
exception. The same applies to a failing `onCompletion` that runs before the consumer (`modeBeforeConsumer`) and a
failing saga compensation, whose exceptions are also added as suppressed exceptions.

The load balancer also no longer fails over when one of the exceptions to fail over on is only the suppressed exception
of the exception the attempt fails with in these cases:

* An `onException` block (or another error handler) fails while it handles the exception: the exchange fails with the
  new exception, and the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception (by the
  `FatalFallbackErrorHandler`). Unlike the `doFinally` case, this is not a 4.23 regression: it changes behaviour that
  dates back to Camel 2.16, intentionally, so that the load balancer matches exceptions as `onException` and `doCatch`
  do.
* The error handler redelivers the attempt (with `inheritErrorHandler` enabled, or the error handler of the route the
  attempt calls) and the last redelivery fails with a different exception than the first attempt: the exchange fails
  with the exception of the last redelivery, and the exception of the first attempt is added to it as a suppressed
  exception. For example, with only `java.io.IOException` in the list, when the first attempt fails with a
  `java.net.ConnectException` and the redelivery with an `HttpOperationFailedException`, the load balancer used to
  fail over and now does not.
* The `onWhen` predicate of a `doCatch` throws an exception: the exchange fails with the exception of the predicate, and
  the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception.
* A processor or component adds a suppressed exception itself (such as Java's try-with-resources when closing a
  resource fails).

To fail over in these cases, add the type of the exception the attempt fails with to the list.

=== camel-core - ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy does not count a failure on a suppressed exception

The exceptions of `ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy` (its `exceptions` option, or the list given to its constructor) are
now matched against the exception the exchange fails with and its causes only, as `onException`, `doCatch` and the
failover load balancer do. Before, they also matched the suppressed exceptions of that exception, so such a failure
counted towards opening the circuit. Since 4.23, an exception thrown in a `doFinally` block is added as a suppressed
exception to the exception from the `doTry` block, so a `doFinally` that threw one of the listed exceptions counted the
failure, although the exception from the `doTry` block was not listed. The same applies, since 4.23, when the `onWhen`
predicate of a `doCatch` throws an exception: the exchange fails with the exception of the predicate, and the original
exception is added to it as a suppressed exception.

A failure also no longer counts when one of the listed exceptions is only the suppressed exception of the exception the
exchange fails with in other cases, for example:

* An `onException` block (or another error handler) fails while it handles the exception: the exchange fails with the
  new exception, and the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception (by the
  `FatalFallbackErrorHandler`).
* The error handler redelivers and the last redelivery fails with a different exception than the first attempt: the
  exchange fails with the exception of the last redelivery, and the exception of the first attempt is added to it as a
  suppressed exception.
* An `onCompletion` that runs before the consumer (`modeBeforeConsumer`) or a saga compensation fails, or a processor or
  component adds a suppressed exception itself (such as Java's try-with-resources when closing a resource fails).

Unlike the `doFinally` and `onWhen` cases, these are not 4.23 regressions; they change older behaviour intentionally, so
that the policy matches exceptions as `onException` and `doCatch` do.

To count these failures, add the type of the exception the exchange fails with to `exceptions`, or leave `exceptions`
empty to count every failure.

=== camel-core - JsonObject and JsonArray convert to their JSON text

The camel-util-json types `org.apache.camel.util.json.JsonObject` and `JsonArray` (given by `convertBodyTo: JsonObject`,
graphql, kubernetes-custom-resources and typesafe-ai) now have type converters to `String`, `byte[]` and
`InputStream` that give their JSON text, using the charset of the exchange for the bytes. Before, a `String` was the
`toString` of the map or list (`{sku=CAMEL-MUG, qty=3}`, which is not JSON), and there was no conversion to `byte[]`
or `InputStream`, so such a body sent to http or a file failed. A `JsonObject` or `JsonArray` that holds a value that
is not JSON, such as a POJO, converts as before.

=== camel-core - TypeConversionException gives the value that failed to convert

When a type converter fails during the conversion of a message, the `TypeConversionException` now gives the value that
failed to convert from `getValue()`, and its type from `getFromType()` and in the message (`Error during type
conversion from type: ...`). Before, they gave the value as a `String` prepared for logging, so the message always
said `from type: java.lang.String`. The message does not include the value itself.

The conversion of a collection or array to an array whose component type does not fit an element, such as a list of
strings to `int[]` or `byte[]`, now gives no conversion (`null`, or `NoTypeConversionAvailableException` for a
mandatory conversion), and other type converters can try. Before, it failed with a `TypeConversionException` due to
`IllegalArgumentException: argument type mismatch` or an `ArrayStoreException`.

=== camel-core - after the doTry called a route that failed, a doCatch clears the failure marks of the exchange

When the `doTry` block calls a route (such as with `direct`) that fails without handling the failure (no `onException`
handles it, and no dead letter channel), the error handler of a route called from the `doCatch` block now runs as it
does anywhere else, as when the `doTry` failed in the same route: it redelivers, runs its `onException` (also one that
does not handle the failure) and its dead letter channel, and logs the exhausted failure. Before, it gave the failure
straight back, and the exchange failed with that route's exception after one attempt. When that route handles its
failure, the rest of the `doCatch` block and the steps after the `doTry` do not run, as for any failure that a called
route handles; when it continues its failure, they run. When the `doCatch` block does not call a failing route, the
route continues after the `doTry` as before.

The exchange no longer keeps the marks of the caught failure, which also changes the following:

* the same applies to a later `doTry` in the same route, and to a `doTry` inside an `onCompletion`;
* the same applies to a route called later from a `split`, `multicast`, `recipientList`, `enrich` or `wireTap` (their
  copies of the exchange carried the mark that the caught failure was not handled);
* a `doTry` and `doCatch` inside an `onException(...).handled(true)`, whose `doTry` calls such a route, no longer makes
  the exchange fail with the exception the `doCatch` caught;
* with `bridgeErrorHandler=true` on the consumer, a failure of such an exchange that is not handled (a `doCatch` that
  fails, or a later failure in the route) now goes to the route's error handler, as an exchange with the body
  `Error processing exchange`, instead of only being logged;
* as when the `doTry` failed in the same route, the error registry (`camel get error`) now also lists the failure that
  such a `doCatch` caught, and the JMX `failuresHandled` counters of the steps inside the `doCatch` block no longer
  count it.

To keep the exchange failing after one attempt as before, give the route called from the `doCatch` no error handler
(`noErrorHandler()`); this applies to every caller of that route.

After the `doCatch` block, the exchange is no longer marked as failure handled either (CAMEL-26447), as when the
`doTry` failed in the same route. So the error handlers that come later handle a new failure as usual (they redeliver,
run their `onException` and dead letter channel, and log an exhausted failure): an `onException` of the calling route
for a failure after the `doTry`, the error handler of a route called later (with a plain `to`, or from the
`doFinally`), and the dead letter channel of a calling route when the `doCatch` block throws. Before, they treated the
exchange as done, and the new failure went back to the caller unhandled. When such an error handler now handles the
new failure, the exchange completes, so its consumer commits or acknowledges the message, as for any handled failure.

The readers of the mark at the end of the exchange now see a message that was processed, as for a `doTry` that failed
in the same route:

* the Idempotent Consumer confirms the message id instead of removing it, so the message is not processed again;
* a resumable consumer records the offset of the message;
* the `failuresHandled` counters (JMX, and the Micrometer and OpenTelemetry route policies) no longer count it;
* with `exactlyOnce` on a Kafka consumer, the output transaction is committed together with the offsets (as the Kafka
  component documents for sends in a `doCatch` block) instead of being aborted, unless the caught failure was a Kafka
  send failure.

There is no option for the old end-of-exchange outcome. For the Idempotent Consumer, throw an exception from the
`doCatch` block (for example with `throwException`): the exchange then fails (or an error handler of the route handles
the new failure), and with `removeOnFailure` (the default) the message id is removed, so the message is processed again
when it comes again.

=== camel-core - Simple: more predicates inside ${ } are read as predicates

A predicate written inside the braces (CAMEL-24921, 4.23) is now also read as one when it uses the operators
`equals`, `!equals`, `!startsWith` or `!endsWith`, and when its left side is a function with a quoted argument, such
as `+${substringAfter('ID-') == '42'}+`. Before, the whole text was read as one function:
`+${header.type equals 'gold'}+` answered null (a predicate that never matches) and `+${uppercase('a') == 'B'}+`
answered `A` (a predicate that always matches). The condition of a ternary inside the braces accepts `equals` and
`!equals` as well: `+${header.type equals 'gold' ? 'a' : 'b'}+` was refused. The same predicates written outside the
braces, such as `+${header.type} equals 'gold'+`, are unchanged.

=== camel-core - Simple: the elvis operator inside the braces

The elvis operator can now be written inside the braces: `${header.limit ?: 10}` means `${header.limit} ?: 10`.
In an expression this used to fail with `${ cannot accept ?:`. In a predicate it used to be read as one function whose
name took in the rest of the text, such as a header named `limit ?: 10`, which is not set: so
`${header.limit ?: 10} == 10` was false, and is true now. Without a space on both sides of `?:` nothing changes:
`${header.limit ?:10}` still reads a header named `limit ?:10`. The default value of a property placeholder,
`${properties:key:a ?: b}`, is not affected.

=== camel-core - Simple: iif and val return a quoted literal without its quotes

In the Simple language, `+${iif(${header.foo} > 0,'positive','negative')}+` returns `positive` again, as it did before
Camel 4.10.7 and 4.13.0 (CAMEL-22204, which made it return `+'positive'+`) and as the ternary operator
`+${header.foo > 0 ? 'positive' : 'negative'}+` does. `+${val('Hello')}+` returns `Hello`; it returned `+'Hello'+`
since `val` was added in Camel 4.18.1. A route that removed the quotes itself, or that relied on them, must be
adjusted. Values without quotes, functions such as `+${body}+`, and the quotes inside the predicate of `iif` are not
changed.

=== camel-core - Simple: a space after a comma is not part of a function argument

The arguments of the `replace`, `substring`, `substringBefore`, `substringAfter`, `substringBetween` and `contains`
functions no longer include the white space around them, as for the collection functions since 4.23 (CAMEL-24969):
`+${replace('o', '0')}+` replaces `o` with `0` (it replaced it with `++ 0++`), and the documented
`+${replace(&quot; , &apos;)}+` replaces double quotes with single quotes. The spaces inside quotes are kept, and an
argument of only white space is kept as it is, so `+${replace( ,-)}+` still replaces a space. A route that relied on a
space next to the comma, such as `+${replace(a,b )}+` for the text `++b ++`, quotes it instead: `+${replace(a,'b ')}+`.
Only the quotes around a whole argument are removed: a quote inside an argument, as in `+a'b'c+`, is now kept (it was
removed).

The prefix and the expression of the `join` function no longer include the white space around them either:
for the body `[A, B, C]`, `+${join(&, id=)}+` gives `id=A&id=B&id=C` (it gave `++ id=A& id=B& id=C++`). An unquoted
separator is kept as written, as it can be white space on purpose: `+${join(; )}+` still joins with `++; ++`; a quoted
separator is the text inside its quotes (`+${join('&' ,id=)}+` joins with `&`, it joined with `++& ++`; put the space
inside the quotes to keep it: `+${join('& ',id=)}+`). A prefix with a space at its start or end is quoted instead:
`+${join(&,' id=')}+`. As for the other functions, only the quotes around a whole argument of
`join` are removed: a quote inside an argument, as in `+${join(&,a'b'c)}+`, is now kept (it was removed), so an
expression such as `+${join(&,id=,${header.x.split(',')})}+` now works.

=== camel-core - Simple: an empty function argument is kept, and a comma in a nested function does not split

In the arguments of `replace`, `substring`, `substringBefore`, `substringAfter`, `substringBetween`, `contains`, `join`,
`split`, `sort`, `setHeader`, `setVariable`, `forEach`, `filter`, `listAdd`, `listRemove`, `mapAdd` and `mapRemove`, an
argument left out now stays in its place instead of letting the next argument take it: `+${replace(b,,${header.s})}+`
replaces `b` with nothing in the header (it replaced `b` with the text `+${header.s}+`), and `+${join(,id=)}+` joins
with a comma and the prefix `id=` (it joined with `id=`). Where the function has no default for it, an argument left out
is refused, so a stray comma that was ignored is now an error, such as `+${contains(xyz,)}+`, `+${substring(,2)}+` or
`+${replace(a,b,${body},)}+`: remove the comma. `+${sort(true,)}+` now sorts the text `true`, and `+${join( )}+` joins
with a space (it joined with a comma). A comma inside a nested function, such as `+${header.s.substring(1,3)}+`, no
longer separates two arguments; a parenthesis outside a nested function is text, as before
(`+${substringBetween(${body},(,))}+`). In the values of `distinct`, `reverse`, `shuffle`, `list` and `map`, a comma
after an unquoted `(` or `{` no longer separates two values: quote a value with a lone parenthesis. A lone quote at the
end of an argument is now kept: `+${replace(b,x')}+` replaces with `x'`.

=== camel-core - Simple: the other functions with arguments keep an empty argument too

The rules of the entry above now also apply to `concat`, `pad`, `convertTo`, `iif`, `hash`, `assert`, `throwException`,
`function`, `range`, `random`, `sum`, `max`, `min` and `average`: a comma inside a nested function, such as
`+${header.s.substring(1,3)}+`, no longer separates two arguments, and an argument left out stays in its place. Where
the function has a default for it, that default is used: `+${concat(,x,-)}+` gives the body, `-` and `x` (it gave `x-`),
`+${hash(,SHA3-256)}+` hashes the body with SHA3-256 (it hashed the text `SHA3-256`). Where it has none it is refused,
such as `+${throwException(,java.io.IOException)}+`, `+${concat(x,)}+` or `+${function(,f)}+`, and a stray comma after
the last argument that was ignored is now an error, such as `+${iif(p,a,b,)}+` or `+${hash(v,SHA-1,)}+`: remove the
comma (which gives the result of before for each of these examples). A lone quote at the end of an argument is now kept,
as in the entry above. An empty value of `sum`, `max`, `min` or `average` is still no value. `+${hash()}+` now hashes
the body with SHA-256, as documented (it was refused).

=== camel-core - a route called from a doTry uses the error handler of its EIPs

A route that a `doTry` calls from its steps, or from its `doCatch` or `doFinally` blocks (with `direct`, `seda`,
`enrich` and so on), now uses the error handler per output of its routing slip, dynamic router, multicast and recipient
list EIPs, as it does when it is called from outside a `doTry`. Before, these EIPs left out that error handler while the
caller was in its `doTry`: a routing slip redelivered the whole slip (so an endpoint that had already succeeded got the
message again), a multicast or recipient list did not redeliver the failed output, and a dynamic router that counts its
calls could lose the failure. The same applies to a route called from the steps of a circuit breaker. A redelivery now
starts at the failed output, so such a route can make more delivery attempts than before.

When the called route handles failures (a dead letter channel, or an `onException` with `handled(true)`), a failed
output of its multicast is now handled there (after the redeliveries of that error handler), and no longer reaches the
`doCatch` of the caller.

To turn off the error handler per output of such an EIP again (no redelivery of its outputs, and the failure goes to the
caller), put the EIP in a `doTry` with only a `doFinally` in the called route, or turn off the error handler of the
called route with `errorHandler(noErrorHandler())`, which turns it off for the other steps of that route too. Neither
option brings back the redelivery of a whole routing slip or dynamic router.

=== camel-core - an Error of an advice of a route, a route policy or the result processor of a send

An `Error` that is not a `VirtualMachineError` (such as a `NoClassDefFoundError` of a data format), thrown by an advice
of a route or of a step (such as the binding of the Rest DSL, `variableReceive`, or the debugger, for example an
assertion in `debugBefore` or `debugAfter` of camel-test), by the result processor of a `ProducerTemplate` send, or by
the producer of a transacted `ProducerTemplate` send, now fails the exchange (wrapped in a `CamelExecutionException`),
and the advices that already ran end as for an exception (such as the end of the unit of work). As for an exception of
an advice, the error handler of that route does not get it. An `Error` of a `RoutePolicy` (`onExchangeBegin`,
`onExchangeDone`) is logged at WARN and ignored, as an exception already was, so an assertion inside a `RoutePolicy`
no longer fails the caller. Before, the `Error` was thrown on to the code that ran the route or the send (such as the
consumer, or the caller of a `ProducerTemplate`), and the exchange could stay inflight (an asynchronous send with a
result processor could never complete when the endpoint answered on another thread). A `VirtualMachineError` is left
as before.

=== camel-core - an AsyncProcessor that throws in a step without an error handler fails the exchange

A step that has no error handler of its own (the steps of `doTry`, `doCatch`, `doFinally` and `onException`, also when
nested in another EIP inside them, the outputs of a multicast, the recipients of a recipient list inside a `doTry`,
the steps of a circuit breaker, and a step with `inheritErrorHandler(false)`) now handles a processor that throws from `process(exchange, callback)` instead of
setting the exception on the exchange as if it had set it: the exchange fails with that exception, so the `doCatch`
catches it, and the output of a multicast is redelivered by the error handler of the route as for any other failure.
Before, the exchange hung.

When such a processor handed its callback to another thread and then throws, the exchange now fails with the thrown
exception unless that thread calls the callback first, and a later call is ignored and logged at WARN; before, the
exception was dropped and that callback continued the exchange as a success. A second call of the callback is ignored
and logged at WARN; before, the exchange was continued a second time. There is no option for the old behaviour; fix the
processor to set the exception on the exchange and call the callback, as an `AsyncProcessor` must.

=== camel-core - an Error of an on completion is logged, and the other on completions run

An `Error` that is not a `VirtualMachineError` (such as an `AssertionError` or a `NoClassDefFoundError`), thrown by an
on completion (`Synchronization`) of an exchange or by its `onBeforeRoute` / `onAfterRoute` callback, is now logged at
WARN and ignored, as an exception already was. The other on completions still run (such as the commit or rollback of
the consumer) and the exchange is no longer left inflight. Before, the `Error` was thrown on to the code that ran the
on completions (such as the consumer, or the caller of a `ProducerTemplate`). An assertion inside an on completion no
longer fails the caller: check its outcome in the route or the test instead.

=== camel-core - an Error of a contract transformer or validator, or in a transacted step, fails the exchange

An `Error` that is not a `VirtualMachineError` (such as an `AssertionError` or a `NoClassDefFoundError`) now fails the
exchange as an exception does (wrapped in a `CamelExecutionException`), also when it is thrown:

* by a transformer or validator of the input or output type of a route (`inputType`, `outputType`). Before, it was
  thrown to the caller of the route without the end of the unit of work, so the exchange could stay inflight;
* by a plain `Processor` of a transacted exchange in a step without an error handler of its own (such as in a `doTry`,
  `doCatch` or `doFinally` block). Before, a `doCatch` did not get it, and it was thrown to the caller;
* by the `AggregationStrategy` of an `enrich` or `pollEnrich` (as a `CamelExchangeException` caused by it, as for an
  exception of the strategy), and the resource is released as failed. Before, for `enrich` the `Error` was thrown to the
  caller and the exchange stayed inflight; for `pollEnrich` the resource (such as a polled file) was not released;
* by the watermark expression of a `split`: it fails that item, as an exception does. Before, it was thrown to the
  caller and the split exchange stayed inflight.

A `VirtualMachineError` is left as before. See the next section.

=== camel-core - an Error thrown by a Processor fails the exchange

When a plain `Processor` (in `process(...)`, in `bean(...)` for a bean that is a `Processor`, or as a producer that is
not an `AsyncProcessor`) throws an `Error` that is not a `VirtualMachineError`, such as an `AssertionError`, the
exchange now fails with it, as for an exception. Before, only a `LinkageError` thrown in `process(...)` and an
`AssertionError` thrown by a bean that is a `Processor` did so (as a bean method that throws any `Error` already does).
Any other `Error` was thrown on after the processor's callback had been called: depending on the error handler, it was
only logged and the next steps ran as if the processor had succeeded, or it reached the caller and the exchange stayed
inflight until the context stopped (which then waited for its shutdown timeout).

[cols="2,4",options="header"]
|===
|Thrown by the processor |What happens

|An `Error` that is not a `VirtualMachineError`, such as `AssertionError`, `LinkageError`
(`NoClassDefFoundError`, `ExceptionInInitializerError`) or a custom `Error`
|The exchange fails with it (wrapped in a `CamelExecutionException`), and the error handler handles it as any other
failure: redelivery, `onException`, `doCatch` and a dead letter channel get it. `onException(Exception.class)` and
`doCatch(Exception.class)` also match it; to treat it differently, match it on its class, such as
`onException(AssertionError.class)`

|A `VirtualMachineError`, such as `OutOfMemoryError`, `StackOverflowError` or `InternalError`
|Not changed by this entry: Camel's error handling does not get it (the JVM may no longer be in a consistent
state, so the application should not handle it). It is not set on the exchange, but thrown on after the processor's
callback was called. Depending on the error handler, the `Error` reaches the caller (and the exchange may stay
inflight), or it is only logged and the route continues as if the processor had succeeded
|===

An application with strict reliability needs should not rely on Camel to react to a `VirtualMachineError`. For an
`OutOfMemoryError` that the JVM raises, it can let the JVM stop with `-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError` or
`-XX:+CrashOnOutOfMemoryError` (these options do not cover a `StackOverflowError`, or an `OutOfMemoryError` that code
throws itself).

`template.send(...)`, `template.request(...)` and `template.asyncSend(...)` return the exchange (or its future) with the
exception and do not throw it. `sendBody(...)` and `requestBody(...)` throw a `CamelExecutionException` that holds the
`Error`, unless the failure was handled (such as by a dead letter channel, `onException(...).handled(true)` or
`doCatch`). A test that sends with `template.send(...)` and asserts only inside a processor
must now check the exception of the returned exchange (`exchange.getException()`), or send with `sendBody(...)` or
`requestBody(...)`.

The same now applies to an `Error` that is not a `VirtualMachineError` thrown by the expression of a `recipientList`, by
the expression of a `split` with `synchronous=true` (or while a `multicast` or `recipientList` with `synchronous=true`
prepares its parts), by the `AggregationStrategy` of a `split`, `multicast` or `recipientList` with `synchronous=true`
and without `parallelProcessing`, by a data type transformer (`transform` to a data type), and by the producer of a
component built on the API component framework when it is not thrown by an API method called by reflection (for example
when the producer creates its client, or calls a client method directly); an `Error` of an API method called by
reflection already failed the exchange. Before, depending on the error handler, the route continued as if the step had
succeeded, or the `Error` reached the caller (for the producer of an API component, the `Error` was lost on its thread).

There is no option for the old behaviour. See also
xref:error-handler.adoc#_an_error_thrown_by_a_processor[An Error thrown by a processor].

=== camel-core - variableReceive into a route or group variable stores the header variables in that route or group

With `variableReceive` (`toV`, `enrich`, `pollEnrich`, `fromV` and so on) into a route variable or a group variable,
the received headers are now stored in the route (group) of the variable. Before, they were stored under a route
(group) with the id `header`, so they could only be read with an internal name, and were not removed with the route.

[width="100%",cols="1m,2m,2m",options="header"]
|===
| Variable | Header variable before | Header variable now
| route:resp (in route rs) | route:header:rs:resp.status | route:header:resp.status or route:rs:header:resp.status
| group:myGroup:resp | group:header:myGroup:resp.status | group:myGroup:header:resp.status
|===

Inside a route, `route:header:resp.status` now means the header variable of the route variable `resp` of the current
route, not the variable `resp.status` of a route with the id `header`.

=== camel-util - FilterIterator.remove after hasNext throws IllegalStateException

`FilterIterator.hasNext()` looks ahead in the iterator it wraps, so a `remove()` after `next()` and then `hasNext()`
removed the element after the one `next()` returned, and a `remove()` before any `next()` removed an element as well.
`remove()` now throws `IllegalStateException` unless it directly follows `next()`. Code that called `hasNext()`
between `next()` and `remove()` must call `remove()` before `hasNext()`.

The same applies to a `remove()` after a `next()` that failed after it looked at further elements: a
`NoSuchElementException` because the filter rejected all the elements that were left (or they were null), or an
exception thrown by the filter or by the iterator it wraps. Such a `remove()` could remove an element that `next()`
never returned; it now throws `IllegalStateException` and removes nothing. A `next()` that fails at the end of the
wrapped iterator, without looking at any element, still leaves the element returned before it removable.

=== camel-util - CaseInsensitiveMap merges keys that differ only in the case of a supplementary letter
