All `vertx-websocket` consumers on the same host and port are served by one HTTP server, which is created from the
`sslContextParameters` of the first consumer to start. A consumer started later with different `sslContextParameters`
(including one with none while the server uses some, or the other way round) used to start and be served with the
first consumer's settings. Such a consumer now fails to start with an `IllegalArgumentException` naming the host and
port. Give every consumer on a host and port the same `SSLContextParameters` instance (for example the same bean
reference, or the global SSL context parameters), or move consumers that need other settings to another port.

=== camel-as2 - the asynchronous MDN allow-list matches the host and port

The `asyncMdnAllowedHosts` option now matches the full authority - host and port - of the asynchronous MDN delivery
address, rather than the host alone. Each entry is a host with an optional port in `host:port` form (IPv6 literals use
bracket notation, for example `[::1]` or `[::1]:8443`). A bare-host entry (for example `partner.example`) now matches
only the default port for the scheme (80 for `http`, 443 for `https`); to deliver to a non-default port, list it
explicitly (for example `partner.example:8443`).

A deployment that lists a bare host and receives asynchronous MDNs on a non-default port must add the port to the entry.

=== camel-asterisk - QUEUE_STATUS and SIP_PEERS return the events of the list

The `QUEUE_STATUS` and `SIP_PEERS` actions of the `asterisk` producer now wait for the whole list and set the
`org.asteriskjava.manager.ResponseEvents` as the body: `getResponse()` is the response and `getEvents()` the
events, such as `QueueParamsEvent`, `QueueMemberEvent` and `PeerEntryEvent`. Before, the body was only the
`ManagerResponse` saying that the list will follow, and the events were lost. A route that read that response from
the body now finds it in `${body.response}`. `EXTENSION_STATE` still sets the `ManagerResponse` as the body.

=== camel-jbang - camel export no longer deletes the files of a directory that no export created

`camel export --dir=<dir>` to a directory other than the current one used to delete the files of that directory first,
whatever they were. It now only does so for a directory that an earlier export created: the export writes a
`.camel-export` file in it. Exporting to a directory with other files fails, and keeps them:

----
Cannot export to /home/me/project because it has files that camel export did not create: use --clean-dir to delete them, or export to another directory
----

This includes exporting to the directory of the exported files themselves from another directory (such as
`camel export /work/route.camel.yaml --dir=/work`), which deleted the source files: use `--clean-dir --yes` for that.
A directory that an earlier Camel version exported to has no `.camel-export` file yet: export to it once with
`--clean-dir --yes`. An empty directory, one with only hidden files (such as `.git`), and the current directory are
exported to as before.

=== camel-jbang - camel validate and camel overview report the same endpoints no route consumes

`camel validate source` and `camel overview` now use the same rules for a `direct:`, `seda:` or `disruptor:` endpoint
that a route sends to and no route consumes, so they report the same endpoints on the same project:

* `disruptor:` endpoints are checked too, and `camel overview` no longer treats `disruptor:q` as `seda:q` (they are
  different endpoints at runtime); `vm:` and `direct-vm:`, which do not exist in Camel 4, are no longer joined with
  `seda:` and `direct:`.
* `camel validate` counts a `poll` or `pollEnrich` as consuming its endpoint, and a `toD` without an expression, an
  `enrich` with a constant endpoint, a rest verb, and the dead letter channel of an error handler as sending to it.
  It reads the flows of an `Integration` resource, and stays quiet for a `Pipe`.
* `camel overview` stays quiet, as `camel validate` does, when a route consumes an endpoint only known at runtime
  (`+from: direct:{{name}}+`), and no longer reports a `to` whose uri holds an expression.

So `camel validate` can report an endpoint it did not report before.

=== camel-jbang - the AI file tools stay inside the project directory, also through a symbolic link

The file tools of `camel mcp`, `camel ask` and the TUI AI agents (`camel_validate_source`, `camel_get_files`,
`camel_edit_file`, `camel_write_file`) now resolve symbolic links before they check that a file is inside the project
directory. A file reached through a link in the project that leads out of it, or through a link to nothing, is
refused for reading and for writing, where it used to be read or written. Name the files of such a folder through
its real directory instead. An absolute `file` inside the project directory is now accepted, as the same file named
relative to it.

=== camel-ftp, camel-jsch, camel-mina-sftp - the SFTP and SCP clients negotiate the post-quantum key exchange

`camel-ftp` and `camel-mina-sftp` now declare `org.bouncycastle:bcprov-jdk18on` as a compile dependency, and
`camel-jsch` picks it up through `camel-ftp`.

Both SSH stacks propose a hybrid post-quantum key exchange ahead of the classical groups - `mlkem768x25519-sha256`
for jsch; `sntrup761x25519-sha512`, also under its `sntrup761x25519-sha512@openssh.com` name, then
`mlkem768x25519-sha256`, `mlkem1024nistp384-sha384` and `mlkem768nistp256-sha256`, in that order, for Apache MINA
SSHD - but both implement ML-KEM through the Bouncy Castle lightweight API (jsch can also use the JDK from Java 24
on). On the Java 21 baseline neither had it on the runtime classpath, so the hybrid groups never reached the
proposal and every SFTP or SCP connection silently fell back to a classical key exchange.

These endpoints therefore now perform a different key exchange than before against a server that offers a hybrid
group. Which one depends on the server: OpenSSH before 9.9 offers only `sntrup761x25519-sha512@openssh.com`, which
`mina-sftp` negotiates and `sftp` does not propose unless it is added to `keyExchangeProtocols`; OpenSSH 9.9 and
later also offers `mlkem768x25519-sha256`, which `sftp` negotiates, while `mina-sftp` still negotiates
`sntrup761x25519-sha512` because it proposes that first. A deployment that must keep the previous behaviour can pin
the classical groups with `keyExchangeProtocols` on `sftp` and `mina-sftp`, and with `kex` on `ssh`. `scp`
(`camel-jsch`) has no such option: there the proposal is set for the whole JVM with the `jsch.kex` system property,
for example `-Djsch.kex=curve25519-sha256,ecdh-sha2-nistp256,diffie-hellman-group16-sha512`. Note that these
options replace the proposal rather than adding to it, so an endpoint that already pins a list of classical groups
is unaffected - and will not negotiate a post-quantum exchange until a hybrid group is added to that list.

With Bouncy Castle on the classpath, Apache MINA SSHD also registers it as the security provider of all its
cryptography in `camel-mina-sftp`, not only of the key exchange: key factories, key pair generation, key agreement,
signatures and digests now come from Bouncy Castle rather than from the JDK providers, which matters on a JVM
configured for FIPS. The registration is switched off with the `-Dorg.apache.sshd.security.provider.BC.enabled=false`
system property.

=== camel-ftp, camel-mina-sftp, camel-jsch, camel-smb, camel-azure-files - checksumFileAlgorithm writes a checksum again

Since 4.18 the producers of the remote file components (ftp, ftps, sftp, mina-sftp, scp, smb, azure-files) accepted
`checksumFileAlgorithm` but wrote no checksum file and did not set the `CamelFileChecksum` header; when an earlier
file producer of the route had set that header, they wrote its checksum, the checksum of another payload. They now
calculate the checksum of the bytes they upload, write the checksum file (unless `checksumWriteFile=false`) and set
the header, as in 4.14. A checksum header set before the producer is no longer used by any file producer. scp and
azure-files upload the bytes of the body without the `charset` option, while the checksum applies it: do not set
`charset` with `checksumFileAlgorithm` there. FTP uploads in ASCII mode unless `binary=true`, and the server may then
change the line ends of the stored file, so its checksum can differ from the checksum of the uploaded bytes.

=== camel-jsch - the scp producer refuses a file name with a line feed or a NUL character

The `scp` producer now refuses a file name with a line feed or a NUL character, in the file name or in one of its
directories, with a `GenericFileOperationFailedException`. It does so before it starts scp on the remote host, so
nothing is written there. Any other character, such as a carriage return or a tab, is sent as before.

scp sends each name on a protocol line that ends with a line feed. Before, a line feed in the file name ended that
line early: the remote scp wrote a file named after the part of the name before the line feed, with the rest of the
name at the start of its content, and the exchange did not fail. A line feed in a directory failed the exchange, but
left a directory named after the part before it. A NUL character failed the exchange on the remote host, or, with an
OpenSSH server before 10.6, cut the name short there. Remove or replace these characters in the `CamelFileName` header
or in the `fileName` option before the message reaches the producer.

=== camel-file - a done file with a fixed name is kept for the files a poll left

A done file with a fixed name (such as `doneFileName=ready`) is no longer deleted while a poll left files of its
directory for the next poll (beyond `maxMessagesPerPoll`, or not started); before, they were left until a new done file
was written. Meanwhile the polls consume every file of the directory, as they do whenever the done file is there. This
also applies to `pollEnrich` and `ConsumerTemplate.receive`, which take one file per call: the done file is now kept
until the last file of the directory is received, where before the first received file deleted it. This applies to
camel-ftp, camel-sftp, camel-smb and camel-azure-files as well.

=== camel-file - poll and pollEnrich evaluate the fileName on the current message

`poll` and `pollEnrich` with a file based component (file, ftp, ftps, sftp, smb and azure-files) evaluate a dynamic
`fileName` that does not use the file functions on the current message, so `+fileName=${body}+` and
`fileName=${body[customer]}.json` read its body. Before, the consumer evaluated it for each file found, with that file
as the body, so `+${body}+` was the file found. A `fileName` that uses the file functions (`${file:...}`,
`${date:file:...}`) is still evaluated for each file found, and headers, variables and exchange properties work as
before. A `fileName` that read the file found through its headers, such as `${header.CamelFileName}`, must use the file
functions instead (`${file:name}`).

=== camel-flink - the datastream endpoint returns a DataStream with the default collect option

A `flink:datastream` endpoint whose callback returns a `DataStream` no longer fails with "collect mode not supported for
Flink DataStreams." when `collect` is not set: the DataStream is the message body and the `CamelFlinkDataStream`
header, as with `collect=false`. An explicit `collect=true` still fails. The `dataStream` and `dataStreamCallback`
options of the flink component are now used by endpoints that do not set them, as `dataSet` and `dataSetCallback`
already were.

=== camel-micrometer - a counter increment or decrement that does not give a number fails the exchange

A `counter` with the `increment` or `decrement` option (such as `+increment=${body}+` or `+increment=${header.amount}+`)
added 1 to the counter when the expression did not give a number, for example a `Map`, a POJO or a `null` body, or a
missing header. It now fails the exchange with an `IllegalArgumentException`, and the counter is not updated. A
counter with neither option still adds 1.

=== camel-mina-sftp - the local work file is deleted when the exchange is done

With `localWorkDirectory`, camel-mina-sftp now deletes the local work file of a consumed file when the exchange is done,
also when it failed, as camel-sftp and camel-ftp do. Before, every consumed file stayed in the local work directory. A
file producer in the route now moves the local work file to its target instead of copying it, while the message body
still names the local work file. So a later step that reads the body again, such as a second file producer or a
`convertBodyTo`, fails because the file is gone, and another exchange that keeps the `java.io.File` body (for example
`wireTap`, `seda` without waiting, or `aggregate`) finds it deleted. Convert the body before the first file producer
or before handing it on, for example with `convertBodyTo(byte[].class)` (which reads the file into memory). Routes that
cleaned the local work directory themselves can stop doing so.

=== camel-olingo4 - filterAlreadySeen keeps the type of a complex or collection value

With `filterAlreadySeen=true`, reading a complex property gave a `java.util.List` of its `ClientProperty` instead of the
`ClientComplexValue`, and an empty `List` when it was read again. A collection property gave a `java.util.List` of the
values not read before instead of a `ClientCollectionValue`. Now a complex value is one result: it is the
`ClientComplexValue`, or `null` when the same value was read before. A collection value stays a `ClientCollectionValue`
with only the values not read before. The consumer sends no message for a poll where nothing new is left in the
collection, as it already did for an entity set. Routes that read the `List` must read the Olingo value instead.

=== camel-openstack - potential breaking change

The four remaining Exchange header constants of `SwiftConstants` have been renamed to follow the Camel naming
convention used by the rest of the component, completing the rename done in 4.21. The Java field names are unchanged;
only the header string values have changed.

[options="header"]
|===
| Constant | Previous value | New value
| `SwiftConstants.CONTAINER_METADATA_PREFIX` | `X-Container-Meta-` | `CamelOpenstackSwiftContainerMetadata`
| `SwiftConstants.VERSIONS_LOCATION` | `X-Versions-Location` | `CamelOpenstackSwiftVersionsLocation`
| `SwiftConstants.CONTAINER_READ` | `X-Container-Read` | `CamelOpenstackSwiftContainerRead`
| `SwiftConstants.CONTAINER_WRITE` | `X-Container-Write` | `CamelOpenstackSwiftContainerWrite`
|===

The previous values were kept in 4.21 on the understanding that they were part of the Swift HTTP contract. They are
not: the container producer passes the values to the openstack4j `CreateUpdateContainerOptions`, which builds the
`X-Container-Meta-`, `X-Versions-Location`, `X-Container-Read` and `X-Container-Write` names of the Swift request
itself. The request Camel sends to Swift is therefore unchanged by this rename.

Routes that reference the constants symbolically (for example `setHeader(SwiftConstants.CONTAINER_READ, ...)`)
continue to work without changes. Routes that set one of these headers by its literal string value (for example
`setHeader("X-Container-Read", ...)` in an XML or YAML route) must be updated to the new value
(`setHeader("CamelOpenstackSwiftContainerRead", ...)`); the previous names are no longer read.

The generated Endpoint DSL header accessors on the Swift `HeaderNameBuilder` are renamed accordingly
(`xContainerMeta()` -> `openstackSwiftContainerMetadata()`, `xVersionsLocation()` ->
`openstackSwiftVersionsLocation()`, `xContainerRead()` -> `openstackSwiftContainerRead()`, `xContainerWrite()` ->
`openstackSwiftContainerWrite()`).

=== camel-core - Split and Multicast child producers start with their route

`Split` and `Multicast` EIPs now start their child output producers during the route's own start, instead of during
`CamelContext` initialization. The visible changes are:

* The producers now start when the route starts, not during `CamelContext` initialization. A `CamelContext` that is
  only built or initialized (without starting routes) no longer starts them.
* Each route's producers now start in `startupOrder` with their route, instead of ahead of all routes.
* The producers of an `autoStartup=false` route are no longer started when the context starts; they start when the
  route itself is started (regardless of which route controller is used).

=== camel-core - Forced route-stop cut-off marks exchange as interrupted in any nested EIP

An exchange that a forced route stop cuts off inside a nested EIP (`split`, `multicast`,
`filter`, `choice`, ...) of the stopped route is now marked as interrupted
(`exchange.getExchangeExtension().setInterrupted(true)`) after the cut-off WARN is logged. This prevents
nested stopped error handlers from each emitting their own WARN for the same exchange. The interrupted flag
also causes the error handler to skip redelivery, `onException` routes, and the dead letter channel — the
same behavior that `DirectProducer` applies (CAMEL-25502) when a route is stopped while an exchange is
waiting for a consumer. A calling route that is still running (for example through `direct`) then also no
longer runs its `onException`, redelivery or dead letter channel for that exchange. If your application
relied on `onException` or the dead letter channel handling a `RouteStoppingException` from a nested EIP
of the stopped route, it will no longer trigger on a forced route stop.

=== camel-opentelemetry-metrics - a counter increment or decrement that does not give a number fails the exchange

A `counter` with the `increment` or `decrement` option (such as `+increment=${body}+` or `+increment=${header.amount}+`)
added 1 to the counter when the expression did not give a number, for example a `Map`, a POJO or a `null` body, or a
missing header. It now fails the exchange with an `IllegalArgumentException`, and the counter is not updated. A
counter with neither option still adds 1. The `CamelMetricsCounterIncrement` and `CamelMetricsCounterDecrement`
headers are documented as `long`, which is how they are read.

=== camel-optaplanner - one SolverManager per endpoint, and consumers get the events of their own CamelContext

With `configFile` and no `SolverManager` (neither the `solverManager` option nor the `CamelOptaPlannerSolverManager`
header), the producer created a new `SolverManager` for every exchange and never closed it, so its threads were never
released. The endpoint now creates one `SolverManager` on first use and closes it when the endpoint stops. As the
exchanges of the endpoint share it, solving a problem while another one with the same `problemId` is still being
solved now fails with `The problemId (1) is already solving`, as it already did with a `SolverManager` you configure.
Use a different `problemId` (an endpoint per problem) to solve several problems at the same time.

A consumer now gets the best solutions of the producers of its own CamelContext only. Before, it also got the ones of
producers in any other CamelContext of the JVM with the same `problemId`. A consumer now also gets the best solutions
of a producer that solves synchronously (`async=false`), not only of the ones that solve asynchronously.

=== camel-file - the checksum of checksumFileAlgorithm is the checksum of the written file

With `checksumFileAlgorithm`, the file producer calculated the checksum from the body before it was written. With
`charset` set, it also decoded that body with the default charset of the JVM instead of the charset of the exchange.
So with `charset`, the checksum (the `CamelFileChecksum` header and the checksum file) did not match the written file
as soon as the encoding changed a byte, a body read with another charset (such as the bytes of a file consumed with
`charset=ISO-8859-1`) was written with its non-ASCII characters replaced, and a String body could fail with an
`UnmappableCharacterException`; with `fileExist=Append` and `appendChars`, the checksum left out the appended
characters. The checksum is now calculated from the bytes written to the file, and the body is decoded as it is
without `checksumFileAlgorithm`. Checksums of such files differ from the ones written before.

=== camel-file - path separator normalization for getFile/directory consistency

On Linux and macOS, a backslash in the `file:` URI path (e.g. `file:target\subdir`) is now normalized
to a forward slash, so that `getFile().getPath()` and `getConfiguration().getDirectory()` return the
same value. Previously, `getFile()` preserved the backslash while `getDirectory()` replaced it with `/`,
which could cause the producer to create a spurious directory with a backslash in its name and the
consumer to miss files in a backslash-named directory. On Windows, where `java.io.File` already
normalizes both separators to `\`, this change has no effect.

=== camel-flatpack - with splitRows each message has its own row, and a failing row fails the producer

With `splitRows=true` (the default), every row message shared the one parsed `DataSet`, so the next row moved the row
of a message that was not yet converted. The body of each row message is now its own read-only `DataSet` with only that
row (`org.apache.camel.component.flatpack.DataSetRow`): it converts to a `Map` as before, converting it to a `List`
gives that one row instead of all the rows, and the methods that change the `DataSet` (such as `setValue` or
`remove`) throw an `UnsupportedOperationException`.

An exception in the route that consumes the rows was lost, and the producer carried on with the next rows. The
exception now fails the producer, and the rows after the failing row are not sent.

Converting a `DataSet` that is not on a row (the whole `DataSet` with `splitRows=false`) to a `Map` now fails with an
`IllegalStateException` instead of a `NullPointerException`; convert it to a `List`.

=== camel-ftp, camel-mina-sftp - the SFTP consumers resolve listed symbolic links for jailStartingDirectory

With `jailStartingDirectory` enabled (the default), the `sftp` and `mina-sftp` consumers now resolve a listed file that
the server reports as a symbolic link, and skip it when its target is outside the starting directory or cannot be
resolved (such as a dangling link), as the file consumer does. A link whose target contains a backslash (`\`) is
skipped too. Before, such a link was consumed like any other file, wherever it pointed. A skipped file is logged at WARN
the first time and at DEBUG on later polls. Links resolving inside
the starting directory work as before. Set `jailStartingDirectory=false` to keep following links to other directories.

=== camel-pg-replication-slot - the LSN of a message is in the CamelPgReplicationSlotLsn header

The consumer no longer sets the exchange id to the log sequence number (LSN) of the message. Several messages can have
the same LSN (for example the `BEGIN` and the first change of a transaction), so exchange ids repeated. The exchange
has a generated exchange id now, as with any other consumer, and the LSN is in the new `CamelPgReplicationSlotLsn`
header (a `String` such as `0/16B3748`). Use `+${header.CamelPgReplicationSlotLsn}+` instead of `+${exchangeId}+` to read
the LSN.

=== camel-pqc - symmetricKeyLength defaults to 256

The `symmetricKeyLength` option of the `pqc` component and of the `pqc` data format now defaults to `256` instead of
`128`. The default KEM is ML-KEM-768, a NIST category 3 algorithm producing a 256-bit shared secret, and deriving a
128-bit key from it threw that margin away.

A route that leaves `symmetricKeyLength` unset therefore now produces a 256-bit key. Two consequences:

* A key encapsulated by 4.24 cannot be used by a peer still running the old default, and material encrypted with the
  `pqc` data format by one version cannot be read by the other. Set `symmetricKeyLength=128` explicitly on both ends
  while they are mixed.
* The algorithms that cannot take a 256-bit key now fail unless `symmetricKeyLength` is set explicitly. Those are
  `RC2` and `CAST5` (40, 64, 128), `DESEDE` (128, 192), and `GRAIN128`, `HC128`, `SEED` and `SM4` (128 only).
  Conversely `CHACHA7539`, `GOST28147`, `GOST3412-2015` and `HC256` accept 256 only, and no longer need an explicit
  value.
+
The `pqc` *component* validates the length when the route starts and fails there with
`Invalid symmetric key length 256 for algorithm <name>. Valid key lengths: [...]`.
+
The `pqc` *data format* does not check the length when it starts, so it fails on the first message instead, when the
cipher rejects the key. Of the algorithms the data format accepts, only `SEED` and `SM4` are affected. Note also that
the algorithm itself can come from the `CamelPQCSymmetricAlgorithm` header, so a route whose configured algorithm is
fine can still fail per message on an override.

=== camel-pqc - an unknown hybridKdfAlgorithm is rejected

The hybrid KEM operations now fail with an `IllegalArgumentException` when `hybridKdfAlgorithm` is not one of
`HKDF-SHA256`, `HKDF-SHA384` or `HKDF-SHA512`. Before, any other value silently fell back to `HKDF-SHA256`, so a typo
such as `HKDF-SHA-512` went unnoticed - and if only one end of an exchange carried the typo, both ends still reported
success while deriving the key with different digests.

The HKDF info bytes of the hybrid KEM are also now encoded explicitly as UTF-8, where they previously used the
platform default charset. The info string is the symmetric algorithm name, so this has no effect for the usual
values such as `AES`, which are pure ASCII. A deployment running on a JVM whose default charset cannot encode that
name identically to UTF-8 would, however, derive a different key than the previous version, and could not decrypt
material produced by it.

=== camel-pqc - a stateful signature key is claimed for one process

XMSS, XMSSMT, LMS and HSS are one-time-signature schemes: signing twice from the same index breaks the key. The index
lives inside the private key, so two processes that load the same key from a shared store - two pods on one volume,
two replicas reading one vault secret - each advance their own copy and hand out the same index.

Before it first signs with a stateful key, the `pqc` producer now claims it from the configured
`KeyLifecycleManager`, through the new `KeyLifecycleManager.claimStatefulKey(String, PublicKey)` and
`releaseStatefulKey(String, PublicKey)` methods. Both have a default implementation, so a custom manager still
compiles; the default grants no claim. The claim is on the key material, identified by the public key (see the new
`KeyLifecycleManager.fingerprint(PublicKey)`), not on the name the `CamelPQCKeyId` header gives the key: the same key
under two names, or under none, is one claim, so an exchange that carries no header is claimed too.

* `FileBasedKeyLifecycleManager` takes an exclusive `FileLock` on a lock file in the key directory named from the
  SHA-256 fingerprint of the public key, so a second process reading that directory is refused.
* `InMemoryKeyLifecycleManager` grants the claim - its keys never leave the process.
* The vault-backed managers (AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault, Hashicorp Vault) cannot offer a lease Camel can
  take, so they do not grant it. **An endpoint signing with a stateful key through one of them now fails**, with a
  message naming the key.

Set the new `allowSharedStatefulKey=true` option on the endpoint to sign anyway, when a single signer is guaranteed
outside Camel (one replica, an external lock, a leader election). A warning is logged once per key.

Nothing changes for the stateless signature algorithms (ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, Falcon and the rest), for the KEM
operations, or for an endpoint that signs with a stateful key and no `KeyLifecycleManager` - that case persists no
index at all and keeps the warning it already had.

=== camel-pqc - the BouncyCastle providers stay registered when an endpoint stops

Stopping a `pqc` endpoint no longer removes the `BC` and `BCPQC` providers from `java.security.Security`.

`java.security.Security` is JVM-global, and the endpoint only added a provider when it was absent, so stopping one
endpoint could remove a provider it had not installed - breaking the other `pqc` endpoint of the same route, other
`CamelContext` instances, components such as camel-crypto, camel-ssh and camel-xmlsecurity, and any non-Camel code in
the same JVM.

An application that relied on the providers being de-registered on shutdown must now remove them itself with
`Security.removeProvider("BC")` and `Security.removeProvider("BCPQC")`.

=== camel-printer - a body that does not convert to a stream fails the exchange

The `lpr` producer submitted a print job with no data, without an error, for a message body that has no conversion to
`InputStream`, such as a `Map`, a POJO or a number, and for a message without a body. It now fails the exchange with an
`InvalidPayloadException`. Send the document as text, bytes, a stream or a file.

=== camel-printer - a doc flavor the print service does not support fails the exchange

The `lpr` producer skipped the print job, without an error, when the print service did not support the doc flavor of
the `flavor` and `mimeType` options, and the exchange completed as if the document was printed. It now fails the
exchange with a `javax.print.PrintException` that names the doc flavor and the print service, also with
`sendToPrinter=false`. Set the `flavor` and `mimeType` options to a doc flavor that the printer supports.

=== camel-debezium - the row flattened by ExtractNewRecordState is the body

With the Debezium `ExtractNewRecordState` single message transformation (`ExtractNewDocumentState` for MongoDB), the
body is now the flattened row (or document) as a `Struct`. Before, the body was `null`, as the component only read the
`after` field of the change event, which the transformation removes. Any record whose value is a `Struct` without an
`after` field is now the body, except the events of Debezium itself (schema change, heartbeat and transaction metadata
events), which keep a `null` body. The `CamelDebeziumOperation` header is read from the `__op` field or header that
the transformation adds with `add.fields=op` or `add.headers=op`.

The `debezium-mongodb` consumer no longer reads the `patch` field for an update, which Debezium removed in 2.0. The
changed fields of an update are now in the new `CamelDebeziumUpdateDescription` header.

The `Struct` to `Map` type converter now also converts a `Struct` inside a list or a map.

=== camel-debezium-mysql - a signed TINYINT column is a Byte

Debezium has been upgraded to 3.7.0.Final, which maps a signed MySQL `TINYINT` column to the Kafka Connect `INT8`
type instead of `INT16` (debezium/dbz#2235). In the `Struct` of the message body, and in the `Map` it converts to,
the value of such a column is now a `Byte` instead of a `Short`. Code that casts the value to `Short` must read it as
a `Number` (or a `Byte`) instead. A `TINYINT UNSIGNED` column is still a `Short`.

The new Debezium options of 3.7 are available on all `debezium-*` components. The `ddlParserType` option of
`debezium-mysql` is kept, although Debezium 3.7 no longer lists it in the configuration of its MySQL connector.

=== camel-docker - the streaming operations give their result, and their failures fail the exchange

The operations `imagebuild`, `imagepull`, `imagepush`, `containerattach`, `containerlog`, `containerwait` and
`execstart` now fail the exchange when they fail. Before, an error such as a missing build context was only logged and
the exchange completed with the body unchanged. A failed build or push, reported by Docker in the output of the
operation, now also fails the exchange.

The body after these operations changed:

* `imagebuild`: the id of the built image, also for a build with a tag. Before, it was `null` for a tagged build.
  The body can now also be a `byte[]` or another body that converts to an `InputStream` (the tar archive of the build
  context), besides an `InputStream` or a `java.io.File`.
* `containerattach`, `containerlog` and `execstart`: the output of stdout and stderr as a `String`. Before, the body
  was the docker-java callback, and the output was lost.
* `containerwait`: the exit code of the container as an `Integer`. Before, it was the docker-java callback.
* `imagepull` and `imagepush`: the last `PullResponseItem` or `PushResponseItem` of the progress. Before, it was the
  docker-java callback.

The `events` consumer now gives the start of the event stream to Docker in seconds, as the Docker API expects, so the
past events of `CamelDockerInitialRange` (in milliseconds) are received. Before, the time was in milliseconds.

`DockerConstants.DEFAULT_CMD_EXEC_FACTORY` now names the Netty factory, which is the default of the `cmdExecFactory`
option. Before, it named the Jersey factory. The default itself did not change; camel-docker now adds the Netty
native transports for arm64, so the default works on Apple silicon and Linux on arm64.

=== camel-pubnub - FIRE and SETSTATE with an empty body fail without calling PubNub

The `FIRE` and `SETSTATE` operations with an empty body now fail with a `CamelException` (`Can not fire empty
message` or `Can not set state with an empty body`) and do not call PubNub, as `PUBLISH` does. Before, the call to
PubNub still ran, so `FIRE` with a null body could end with the `PubNubException` `Message Missing` instead. The
message of the `SETSTATE` failure was `Can not publish empty message`.

The `PNMessageResult` body of the consumer now converts to a `String`: a string message as its text, any other message
as JSON. Before, the conversion gave the `toString()` of the `PNMessageResult` object.

=== camel-jbang - camel validate normalize writes one file per input file to an output directory

`camel validate normalize <files> --output=<dir>` now writes one normalized file per input file into the
directory, with the name of the input file, instead of merging all the routes into `normalized.yaml`. A
top-level `onException` of a file then keeps applying to the routes of that file only, as it does in the
source. This applies to a single input file too: `camel validate normalize orders.yaml --output=out/` now
writes `out/orders.yaml` instead of `out/normalized.yaml`. `normalized.yaml` is only written for what was
not loaded from one of the input files. Input files with the same name in different directories are
written as `<parent directory>-<name>`. Scripts that read `<dir>/normalized.yaml` must read the per-file
outputs instead.

The command now refuses to write over one of its input files (for example `--output=.` in the directory of
the routes) and exits with an error.

With `--output=<file>` or without `--output`, the routes are still merged, and the command now warns, on
the standard error, when the top-level `onException` of one file applies to the routes of the other files
in the merged output.

=== camel-pqc - generateSecretKeyEncapsulation stamps the JCE algorithm name on the secret key

The `SecretKeyWithEncapsulation` produced by `generateSecretKeyEncapsulation` now carries the mapped JCE algorithm
name, as the `extractSecretKeyEncapsulation` and `extractSecretKeyFromEncapsulation` operations already did. Before, it
carried the raw enum name.

This is only visible for the two algorithms whose enum name differs from the JCE name: `DESEDE` now yields `DESede`
(was `DESEDE`), and `GOST3412_2015` now yields `GOST3412-2015` (was `GOST3412_2015`). The raw names are not resolvable
cipher algorithms, so a key labelled with one could not be passed to `Cipher.getInstance(key.getAlgorithm())`, and the
generate and extract ends of the same exchange disagreed on the key's identity. Code asserting on the old value must
be updated.

`generateSecretKeyEncapsulation` now also rejects a missing `symmetricKeyAlgorithm` with
`Symmetric Algorithm needs to be specified`, as the extract operations already did, instead of failing later inside
BouncyCastle with `Missing argument`.

=== camel-pqc - the hybrid KEM operations stamp the JCE algorithm name too

`hybridGenerateSecretKeyEncapsulation` and `hybridExtractSecretKeyEncapsulation` label the `SecretKey` they produce
with the mapped JCE algorithm name, as the non-hybrid operations do. `DESEDE` now yields `DESede` and
`GOST3412_2015` now yields `GOST3412-2015`; every other algorithm is unaffected, since its configured name and its
JCE name are the same.

Only the label changes. The derived key itself is unchanged: the configured name is also the HKDF info the two
shared secrets are combined with, and that keeps its original value, so an encapsulation produced by an earlier
version still decrypts.

=== camel-jbang - camel ask asks before a call that changes something, reaches out or runs code

`camel ask` now asks on the terminal before a file write (showing the diff), a message to an endpoint the
integration does not have, SQL that changes data, an expression in a language that runs code, stopping or
restarting the integration, and a `camel` command that changes something. Without a terminal to ask on, as
in a script, such a call does not run: pass `--yes` to let it run without asking.

The model gets the shared file tools (`camel_get_files`, `camel_edit_file`, `camel_write_file`), which
validate what they write, instead of the `read_file`, `list_files` and `write_file` tools of `camel ask`. A
local model gets a core set of tools (`--tools=auto`, the default), a hosted model the full set. The default
of `--max-iterations` is now 25 instead of 10, and `--model llama3.2` now selects that model also when a
model better at tool calling, such as `qwen3.6`, is installed.

=== camel-pqc - the usage-count and PENDING_ROTATION key rotation signals now fire

Two of the three documented rotation signals of `KeyRotationScheduler` previously never triggered. Both now work, so a
deployment that already configured them will start rotating keys where before nothing happened.

* `maxKeyUsage` (and `camel.component.pqc.key-rotation-max-usage`) read `usageCount`, which was only advanced for the
  stateful signature algorithms (XMSS, XMSSMT, LMS, HSS). For ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, Falcon and the KEM keys the counter
  stayed at zero forever. A signing operation now advances it for every key, provided a `keyLifecycleManager` is
  configured and the exchange carries a `CamelPQCKeyId` header.
* The scheduler's default key filter accepted only `ACTIVE` keys, so a key an operator had marked
  `PENDING_ROTATION` was skipped before `needsRotation()` was consulted. The default filter now accepts `ACTIVE` and
  `PENDING_ROTATION`. A custom filter set with `setKeyFilter` is unaffected.

Review any configured `maxKeyUsage` threshold before upgrading: a long-running deployment may rotate on the first
check once the counter starts advancing.

Recording the usage also costs a write. A signing endpoint with a `keyLifecycleManager` and a `CamelPQCKeyId` header
now updates the key metadata in the backing store on every signature, where a non-stateful key previously wrote
nothing. Against AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault or HashiCorp Vault that is a remote call per message. A failure
to read or write that metadata does not fail the exchange for a non-stateful key - it is logged once per producer and
the signature is returned - because losing a usage count only delays a rotation. Leave the `CamelPQCKeyId` header off
the signing exchanges, or use `maxKeyAge` or `nextRotationAt`, if the write is not wanted.

The key encapsulation operations do not record usage, so `maxKeyUsage` still never triggers for a KEM key.

=== camel-pqc - a failed stateful signature no longer carries the signature headers

Signing with one of the stateful hash-based algorithms (LMS, HSS, XMSS, XMSSMT) consumes a one-time index inside the
private key. That advanced key is now persisted through the `keyLifecycleManager` *before* the signature is placed on
the message, instead of after.

Such an exchange already failed when the key state could not be stored, so what changes is the content of the failed
message: it no longer carries `CamelPQCSignature`, nor the hybrid `CamelPQCHybridSignature`,
`CamelPQCClassicalSignature` and `CamelPQCPqcSignature` headers. A route that caught the failure with
`onException(...).handled(true)`, or routed it to a dead letter channel, could previously read the signature off the
failed message and pass it on; it can no longer do so. That is the point of the change - a signature whose index
advance was never recorded must not escape, because after a restart the key resumes at the previous index and can
sign a second time with an index already used.

Signing a stateful key without a `keyLifecycleManager`, and signing any key without a `CamelPQCKeyId` header or
with no stored metadata for the key, now logs a warning once per producer rather than being silently ignored. A
non-stateful key without a `keyLifecycleManager` stays silent, as there is no `maxKeyUsage` threshold to miss. For a
stateful key the warning names the index-reuse risk; for any other key it names the `usageCount` that will not advance.

=== camel-jooq - a message with no body fails

The jooq producer now fails with an `InvalidPayloadException` when the message body is null and the `query` option is
not set. Before, operation `NONE` (the default) created a new, empty record of the `entityType` and stored it, so a
message with no body inserted an empty row (or failed with a database constraint error). Operations `EXECUTE` and
`FETCH` failed with a `NoSuchMethodException` for `org.jooq.Query.<init>()`. Send the record, or the `org.jooq.Query`, in
the message body. The `operation` option also no longer lists `CONSUME`, which was never a valid value.

=== camel-jsonpath - easy predicates with `+<=+` and `+>=+` match the boundary value

An easy predicate (such as `+store.book.price <= 10+` or `+$.store.book.price >= 10+`) now matches the boundary
value. Before, `+price <= 10+` ran as `+price < 10+` and `+price >= 10+` as `+price > 10+`, so a message with a price of
exactly 10 was not matched, and a content-based router sent it to another branch. A route that worked around this (for
example with `+price <= 10.01+`) can go back to the plain boundary. Also, an operator word inside a quoted value
(`+title == 'Java in Action'+`) is now part of the value, and the type-safe operators `+===+` and `+!==+` are passed to
JsonPath as written (before, they ran as `+==+` and `+!=+`).

=== camel-jbang - camel get endpoint shows the scope of an endpoint

`camel get endpoint` replaces the `REMOTE` column with `SCOPE`, which tells three kinds of endpoint apart:
`remote` (an external system, as before), `internal` (Camel plumbing that stays inside the integration, such as
`direct`, `seda` or `log`, as the catalog marks its component internal) and `local` (neither, such as `exec` or
`bean`). Scripts that read the `REMOTE` column of the table must read `SCOPE` instead. The `--json` output keeps
`remote` and adds `internal`.

=== camel-pqc - the CamelPQCOperation header is validated, and two header types are corrected

A `CamelPQCOperation` header selecting an operation the endpoint holds no key material for is now rejected with an
`IllegalArgumentException` naming the requested operation, the configured one, and what is missing. Before, an
override crossing between the signature and the key encapsulation operations dereferenced a null and surfaced as a
bare `NullPointerException`. Routes relying on such an override have to configure a separate endpoint for the other
operation; the override itself keeps working within a family and for the operations that need no key material.

Two header declarations also carried the wrong `javaType` in the component metadata, which is visible to tooling and
in the generated header table. `CamelPQCOperation` is `org.apache.camel.component.pqc.PQCOperations` (was `String`)
and `CamelPQCSecretKey` is `javax.crypto.SecretKey` (was `Boolean`). The runtime values are unchanged - only the
declared types were wrong.

=== camel-jbang - finding integrations by name or PID

All commands find running integrations the same way:

* A PID given to a command must be a running Camel integration (a live process with a status file in `~/.camel`).
  Before, any PID was used as is, so `camel stop 1234 --kill` killed whatever process had that PID.
* The commands no longer find an integration whose Camel has stopped (phase 9 or later), such as the launcher process
  of an integration that runs in a child process. `camel ps` does not list it anymore.
* The `--timeout` of `camel cmd send`, `camel cmd receive`, `camel eval expression`, `camel transform message` and the
  `camel semantic` commands also takes a duration such as `30s` (a number is still millis).

=== camel-jbang - a Maven repository that does not answer fails the download after 60 seconds

The Camel CLI (`camel run`, `camel export`, the CLI plugins, and other tools that download with `camel-tooling-maven`) now
waits 60 seconds for data from a Maven repository, instead of the 30 minutes of Maven Resolver, so a repository that
stops sending no longer hangs the download for that long. Set the `aether.connector.requestTimeout` system property
(in millis) for a longer timeout. A repository that cannot be connected to is now logged with a WARN and not used
for the rest of the run.

=== camel-pqc - KeyLifecycleManager behaviour aligned across the implementations

Four differences between the `KeyLifecycleManager` implementations are resolved. All four are corrections, but each
is observable.

* `updateKeyMetadata` on the AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault and HashiCorp Vault managers no longer re-stores
  the key pair. It previously read the key back and wrote it again, creating a new version of the private key secret
  on every status change - with the default `max_versions = 10` of a Vault KV v2 mount, ten metadata edits purged
  every historical version of the private key. It also meant `revokeKey` threw for a key whose private secret was
  already gone. The file-based manager already wrote metadata only.
* `FileBasedKeyLifecycleManager.listKeys()` now scans the key directory instead of returning an in-process cache
  filled once at construction. A key file written after start-up - by another process, a restored backup or a
  sidecar - was previously invisible, which silently stopped `KeyRotationScheduler` from rotating it.
* The `deleteKeyState` operation now fails with `IllegalStateException` when no `keyLifecycleManager` is configured,
  as every other key lifecycle operation already did. It previously reported success having deleted nothing.
* `exportKey` with `PEM` and `includePrivate = true` produces a document that `importKey` can read back. The
  combined export carries both a `PUBLIC KEY` and a `PRIVATE KEY` block, and the parser stripped only one pair of
  armour lines, leaving the other in the Base64 input and failing with `Illegal base64 character 2d`. The export
  format itself is unchanged, so documents written by earlier versions are unaffected.
* `importKey` resolves the `KeyFactory` with the BouncyCastle provider that serves the algorithm, as the file-based
  manager's key loading already did. On JDK 24 and later the JDK itself implements ML-DSA and ML-KEM and is listed
  before BouncyCastle, so the provider-less lookup used until now returned a JDK key, and signing with it through the
  component's BouncyCastle `Signature` failed with `unknown private key passed to ML-DSA`. A key imported through the
  AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault, HashiCorp Vault or in-memory manager is now a BouncyCastle key on every JDK.

PEM handling is also now explicitly UTF-8 rather than the platform default charset.

=== camel-jbang - exit codes, and errors on stderr

The Camel CLI has a documented set of exit codes (see xref:camel-jbang-exit-codes.adoc[Exit Codes and Output]):
`0` success, `1` failed, `2` usage error, `3` not found, `66` file not found, `70` internal error and `124` timed out.
A script that checks the exit code of a command must take these changes into account:

* A command that ends with an unexpected exception exits with `70` instead of `1`, and prints `ERROR:`, the message and
  the stack trace on stderr. Add `--verbose` or set `CAMEL_CLI_DEBUG=true` to print the stack trace of other errors too.
* Commands that exited with `255` now exit with `1`, `2`, `3`, `66` or `124`: `camel bind`, `camel debug`,
  `camel dependency update`, `camel eval expression`, `camel infra log`, `camel transform message` and `camel update run`.
  `camel debug` exits with the exit code of the debugged integration when it ends.
* `camel semantic` exits with `124` instead of `4` when the integration does not reply in time.
* `camel generate schema` exits with `2` for an empty argument, `3` when the class is not found, `1` when the schema
  cannot be generated and `70` for an unexpected error, instead of `4`, `2`, `3` and `4`.
* `camel transform dataweave` exits with `2` without `--input` or `--expression`, and with `66` when the input path does
  not exist or holds no `.dwl` files, instead of `1`.
* `camel cmd sql` exits with `124` instead of `1` when the query does not answer in time.
* A source file of `camel cmd route-diagram`, `camel cmd route-structure` and `camel cmd route-topology` that does not
  exist exits with `66` instead of `1`. The parts of a Java route the parser could not read are printed as a warning on
  stderr (also with `--json`, which lists them in `unread` as before).
* A file or directory that does not exist exits with `66` instead of `1`: a source file or `--source-dir` of
  `camel run`, the route file of `camel explain` and `camel harden` (checked before the LLM is contacted), and the
  `pom.xml` of `camel dependency runtime`.
* A name that does not exist exits with `3` instead of `1`: the Kamelet of `camel init --from-kamelet`, the service of
  `camel infra run`, the key of `camel config get`, the name given to `camel doc`, and the plugin of
  `camel plugin delete`.
* `camel cmd memory-leak` and `camel restart` exit with `3` when no running integration, or more than one, matches the
  name. `camel cmd memory-leak` exits with `124` when the integration does not reply in time.
* `camel init` without a file, or with `--from-kamelet` and a file that is not a `.kamelet.yaml` file,
  `camel infra run` with a malformed property, and `camel kubernetes delete` without `--name` exit with `2` instead of
  `1`.
* A command that runs its default sub-command (`camel config`, `camel dependency`, `camel get`, `camel infra`,
  `camel plugin`, `camel top`, `camel update` and `camel version`) exits with the exit code of that sub-command,
  instead of always `0`.
* The first use of the command of a Camel plugin (such as `camel kubernetes`) installs the plugin and runs the command,
  instead of asking to run the command again and exiting with `0`.

Errors, warnings and tips are printed on stderr instead of stdout, so stdout only has the output of the command, such
as JSON. If a script reads the error text of a command from stdout, read stderr instead. The list of plugins that are
not installed, after `camel` and `camel --help` (no longer after the help of a command, such as `camel run --help`),
is printed on stderr, and only in an interactive terminal.

=== camel-pqc - parameterSpec accepts MAYO and SNOVA, and the FIPS advisory no longer flags two aliases

`parameterSpec` can now select a named parameter set for `MAYO` and `SNOVA`. Both were rejected on the grounds that
they have no name-addressable BouncyCastle parameter spec, which is not the case: `MayoParameterSpec.fromName` and
`SnovaParameterSpec.fromName` both exist, and neither algorithm is stateful. The stateful hash-based signatures
(`XMSS`, `XMSSMT`, `LMS`, `HSS`) are still rejected, as they genuinely have no such spec.

`DILITHIUM` and `SPHINCSPLUS` no longer produce the "not part of NIST FIPS 204/205" warning on hybrid endpoints.
Both are aliases that resolve to `ML-DSA` and `SLH-DSA`, so the warning was recommending the algorithm already in
use. `FALCON`, `MAYO`, `SNOVA`, `XMSS` and `XMSSMT` still warn.

The five default key material classes that generated their key pair with the provider's default RNG (`Falcon`,
`MAYO`, `SNOVA`, `LMS`, `HSS`) now use the shared `SecureRandomHelper` like the other sixteen. No key format or
parameter set changes; only the randomness source is now uniform.

=== camel-docker - the connection and credential headers need the allowConnectionAndCredentialsFromHeaders option

The producer of the `docker` component took the Docker daemon to connect to, how to connect to it, and the registry
credentials from the `CamelDockerHost`, `CamelDockerPort`, `CamelDockerSocketEnabled`, `CamelDockerSecure`,
`CamelDockerTlsVerify`, `CamelDockerCertPath`, `CamelDockerCmdExecFactory`, `CamelDockerUsername`,
`CamelDockerPassword` and `CamelDockerServerAddress` headers when they were set, over the options of the endpoint.
These headers are now ignored unless the endpoint sets the new `allowConnectionAndCredentialsFromHeaders=true` option
(disabled by default), and the producer uses the `host`, `port`, `socket`, `secure`, `tlsVerify`, `certPath`,
`cmdExecFactory`, `username`, `password` and `serverAddress` options of the endpoint.

A route that relies on these headers, for example one that sends the registry username and password as headers to
`docker:auth` or `docker:imagepush`, must set `allowConnectionAndCredentialsFromHeaders=true` on the endpoint, or on
the component (`camel.component.docker.allowConnectionAndCredentialsFromHeaders=true`) to apply it to all its
endpoints.

The other headers, such as `CamelDockerContainerId`, `CamelDockerEmail` and `CamelDockerRequestTimeout`, still take
precedence over the options of the endpoint, as before. The `events` and `stats` consumers do not change: they always
used the options of the endpoint.

=== camel-docker - imagepull and imagepush authenticate only to the registry of serverAddress

The `imagepull` and `imagepush` operations of the `docker` component added the registry authentication of the
endpoint (`username`, `password`, `email` and `serverAddress`) to every pull and push, whatever registry the image
reference names. They now add it only when the registry host and port of the image (Docker Hub for an image without a
registry host, such as `ubuntu` or `team/app`) match those of `serverAddress`. An image in another registry is pulled
or pushed without authentication. Set `serverAddress` to the registry of the images, or use one endpoint per registry.

Without a `username` on the endpoint, `imagepull` and `imagepush` failed with a `NullPointerException`
("Configured username is null."). They now pull or push without authentication.

=== camel-cli-connector - every action with a request id gets an answer

An action written to `~/.camel/<pid>-action-<requestId>.json` now always gets an answer in
`~/.camel/<pid>-output-<requestId>.json`, so a tool no longer waits for its timeout:

* an action without a result (such as a route stop) answers `{"status":"success"}`,
* an action that is unknown, throws an exception or reports an error answers
  `{"status":"failed","error":"..."}`, with `"reason":"not-found"` when what it was asked to work on does not exist
  (such as `No route matching: route9`, and the new `No processor matching: ...`).

The WebSocket transport adds the same `reason` to its `result` frame. The shared `~/.camel/<pid>-action.json` used by
older Camel CLI versions is unchanged. A tool built on the file transport that relied on actions without a result
writing nothing, or that waited for a timeout, sees these answers now. The Camel MCP route tools, for example, now
report a route stop as done instead of a timeout.

=== camel-jbang - JSON output

With `--json`, stdout is exactly one JSON document, also when the command fails, so a script or an AI agent can parse
it (see xref:camel-jbang-exit-codes.adoc#_json_output[JSON output]):

* An error prints `{"status":"error","code":3,"message":"..."}` on stdout, with the exit code of the command in `code`,
  and the `ERROR:` line on stderr as before. Before, stdout was empty or had text.
* A list with nothing in it prints `[]` instead of nothing, such as `camel ps`, `camel get ...` and `camel top ...` when
  no integration is running, and `camel catalog ...` when the filter matches nothing. The message about a filter that
  matches nothing is printed on stderr.
* `camel top route --json` and `camel top group --json` print JSON instead of a table, with the load averages in
  `load01`, `load05` and `load15`.
* `camel get route --error --json` prints the routes with an error, and their `lastError`, as JSON instead of tables.
  `camel get health --trace --json` puts the `stackTrace` and `metadata` of a check in the JSON, instead of printing
  them as text after it.
* `camel get count --total --json` and `camel get count --fail --json` print JSON instead of the comma separated counts.
* `camel infra get --json` and `camel infra stop --json` print JSON instead of text, `camel infra run --background --json`
  prints the pid as JSON, and `camel infra run --json` only prints the properties of the service. `camel infra get` and
  `camel infra stop` only see services that are running, and exit with `3` when a name matches none.
* `camel overview --format=json` only prints the JSON on stdout: the `Saved` message of `--save` and `--ai`, and the
  progress and the prompts of `--ai`, go to stderr, and `--ai` no longer prints the summary before the JSON.
  `camel overview --apply-descriptions --format=json` prints the changed files as JSON.
* `camel doctor` exits with `1` when a check fails (Maven cannot resolve artifacts, or more than one installation of the
  Camel CLI is found). A missing container runtime or Ollama, and ports in use, are warnings, which do not fail it.

New `--json` options: `camel doctor` (the status `OK`, `WARN` or `FAIL` of each check), `camel validate yaml`,
`camel validate properties` and `camel validate source` (`valid`, `file` and `errors` of each file, as the MCP tool
`camel_validate_source` answers), `camel doc` (the documentation the MCP tool `camel_catalog_doc` gives),
`camel config get`, `camel config list`, `camel plugin get` and `camel plugin list`.

=== camel-jbang - camel cmd actions report failures and timeouts

The commands that act on a running integration now report how the action went in their exit code (see
xref:camel-jbang-exit-codes.adoc[Exit Codes and Output]):

* An action without a result waits until the integration confirms it, and exits with `1` when it fails, `3` when the
  route or processor does not exist, and `124` when the integration does not answer within `--timeout` (10 seconds by
  default; a number is millis, or use a duration such as `30s`): `camel cmd start-route`, `stop-route`,
  `suspend-route`, `resume-route`, `start-group`, `stop-group`, `enable-processor`, `disable-processor`, `gc`,
  `reload`, `reset-stats`, `logger --logging-level` and `camel trace --action=start/stop`. Before, they wrote the action
  and exited with `0`. An integration on an older Camel version takes the action without confirming it.
* A command that waits for a result exits with `124` when the integration does not answer in time, and with `1` when
  it answers with an error, instead of a message and `1`, or nothing and `0`: such as `camel cmd thread-dump`,
  `heap-histogram`, `heap-dump`, `browse`, `span`, `stub`, `sql`, `load`, `memory-leak`, `route-dump`,
  `route-structure`, `route-diagram`, `route-topology`, `camel get bean`, `source`, `route-controller`,
  `startup-recorder`, `camel top source`, `camel get error --diagram` and `camel get history --diagram`.
  When the integration has no data to show (a thread dump, the route controller, top source), or OpenTelemetry is not
  enabled (`camel cmd span`), the command exits with `1` instead of `0`.
* A name or pid that matches no running integration exits with `3`, instead of doing nothing and exiting with `0`.
  Without a name (all integrations) and nothing running, the command exits with `0` and prints
  `No running Camel integrations` on stderr.
* An integration that stops before it answers the action exits with `3` as soon as it is gone, instead of waiting until
  `--timeout` and exiting with `124`, such as a `camel cmd send` that waits for the reply of a slow route.
* `camel eval expression` with a name or pid that matches no running integration exits with `3`, instead of starting
  a new Camel to evaluate in: only without a name, or with `--isolated`, a new Camel evaluates the expression. An
  evaluation that fails exits with `1`, and the `Using existing running Camel integration` line is printed on stderr.
* `camel cmd send` exits with `1` when the exchange failed and `124` when the endpoint did not answer in time, also
  without a name (sending from a local Camel), which exited with `0` before. `camel transform message` exits with `1`
  when the transformation fails and `124` when it does not answer in time.
* `camel cmd load` without `--source`, a header that is not in the `key=value` form (`camel cmd send`,
  `camel eval expression`, `camel transform message`), and an unknown `--action` (`camel cmd receive`, `camel trace`)
  exit with `2`.
* The `--filter` of `camel cmd route-dump`, `camel cmd route-structure` and `camel get source` takes several names
  separated by comma, as its description says, and a filter that matches no route exits with `3`.

The commands that wait for the integration have a `--timeout` option, and the ones that return data have a `--json`
option: `camel cmd thread-dump`, `heap-histogram`, `heap-dump`, `browse`, `span`, `stub`, `sql`, `logger`,
`route-dump`, `camel get bean`, `source`, `route-controller`, `startup-recorder`, `camel top source` and
`camel trace` (the status of tracing). `camel get history --json` prints one JSON array instead of one document per
line.

=== camel-jbang - camel get, top and ps: not found, unreadable status, and the JSON of the listings

* `camel get ...`, `camel top ...` and `camel ps` with a name or pid that matches no running integration exit with `3`,
  instead of printing nothing and exiting with `0`. Without a name and with nothing running, they exit with `0` and print
  `No running Camel integrations` on stderr (and `[]` with `--json`).
* An integration whose status file a listing cannot read, such as one of an older Camel version without a value the
  listing needs, is skipped with a `WARN:` on stderr, instead of failing the listing of every integration.
* `camel get health` takes a name or pid, like the other listings.
* `camel get endpoint`, `consumer` and `producer --limit` keep the first rows after sorting (they kept the first rows
  found), and `camel get processor --limit` counts routes, as its description says.
* `camel dirty` no longer keeps the files of another pid that starts with the same digits (pid `12` kept
  `123-status.json`), nor removes the files of a process that is alive.

The JSON of the listings (`--json`) changes, so that a script can use the values without parsing text:

* `pid` and the counters are numbers instead of text: `total`, `failed`, `inflight`, `mean`, `min`, `max`, `last`,
  `delta` and `throughput`. A value that is not there is `null`, instead of an empty or `-` text.
* Values that combined several numbers are split: `sinceLast` (`1s/2s/-`) into `sinceLastStarted`, `sinceLastCompleted`
  and `sinceLastFailed`; the `route` of `camel get context` (`3/4`) into `routesStarted` and `routesTotal`; its `reload`
  into `reloaded` and `reloadFailed`; and in `camel top context` the `load` into `load01`, `load05` and `load15`, `heap`
  into `heapUsed`, `heapCommitted` and `heapMax` (bytes), `nonHeap` into `nonHeapUsed` and `nonHeapCommitted`, `gc` into
  `gcCount` and `gcTime` (millis), and `threads` into `threads` and `peakThreads`. In `camel get health`, `rl` becomes
  `readiness` and `liveness`, `rate` becomes `total`, `success` and `failure`, and `since` becomes `sinceLast`,
  `sinceStartSuccess` and `sinceStartFailure`.
* `ready` is a boolean (`camel ps`, `camel get context`), and `camel ps` has `failed` instead of `fail`.
* Next to `age` (such as `5m3s`), the listings have `uptime` in millis. Durations are millis: `duration` and `elapsed` of
  `camel get blocked`, `inflight` and `internal-task`, and `time` of `camel get groovy`, which lists its `classes` as an
  array.
* The id of a route is `routeId` (it was `route` in `camel get blocked`, `circuit-breaker`, `inflight` and `kafka`), and
  the state is `status` (it was `state` in `camel get circuit-breaker`). `camel get consumer` has `total` instead of
  `poll`. Every row of `camel get processor` has the `name` of its integration.

=== camel-jbang - camel get route, processor, metric and health

* `camel get route --error --limit` shows that number of routes with an error (it limited the routes first, and then
  showed the ones with an error among them), and `--note` shows the notes of the routes with an error too.
* `camel get processor --filter` shows the matching processors with their route, and so with the name and pid of their
  integration (it showed the processors alone). `--filter-mean` leaves out the faster processors too, as its
  description says (it left out the faster routes only).
* `camel get metric` shows the distribution summaries: an integration that has one was skipped with a warning.
  `--filter` takes a pattern such as `timer*`, as in its help (it showed nothing), and `--custom` leaves out the metrics
  of Camel in the default naming of Micrometer, such as `camel.exchanges.total`, not only in the classic naming
  (`CamelExchangesTotal`).
* `camel get health --level` other than `full` or `default` exits with `2` (it showed every check, as `full` does).

=== camel-jbang - camel stop and camel restart wait for the integration

* `camel stop` waits until the integration has stopped, up to `--timeout` (60 seconds by default), and exits with `124`
  when it has not stopped in time (use `--kill` to stop it at once). Before, it asked the integration to stop and exited
  with `0` at once; `--no-wait` does that. An integration without a pid file in `~/.camel` is stopped with `SIGTERM`,
  which did nothing before.
* `camel stop` and `camel restart` with a name or pid that matches no running integration exit with `3`.
* `camel restart` waits until the integration has stopped (terminating it when it does not stop within `--timeout`), and
  does not start it again while the old process is alive (exit `124`). It waits until the new integration has started,
  and keeps its output in a log file in `~/.camel`, which it prints (the log was removed when the command ended). A new
  integration that stops right away prints the end of its log and exits with `1`; one that is still starting after
  `--timeout` exits with `124` and is left running.

=== camel-jbang - camel cmd browse of an endpoint that cannot be browsed

`camel cmd browse --endpoint` with an endpoint that the integration cannot browse, or that it does not have, exits with
`3` (it printed an empty table and exited with `0`).

=== camel-jbang - no command waits for a terminal that is not there

Without an interactive terminal, such as in a script, a CI job (the `CI` environment variable is set) or an AI agent:

* `--watch` without `--duration` shows the output once, with a `WARN:` on stderr, instead of repeating until Enter is
  pressed. The new `--duration` option (such as `--duration=30s`) keeps watching for that long, and then exits with `0`.
* `camel log`, `camel trace` and `camel cmd receive` do not follow, unless `--follow` or `--duration` is given.
  `camel infra log` prints the end of the logs and exits, and has `--follow` and `--duration` too.
* `camel hawtio` does not open a web browser, and prints the URL of the web console instead. `--openUrl` is now named
  `--open-url` (the old name still works).
* `camel debug`, `camel nano`, `camel shell`, `camel edit`, `camel tui` (except with `--record`), `camel ask` without a
  question, `camel run --prompt` and `camel get history --it` exit with `2` and `requires an interactive terminal` on
  stderr. `camel debug` used to wait at 100% CPU. `camel debug --background` does not require an interactive terminal.

`--follow` and `--open-url` without a value turn the option on. As boolean options that are on by default, they turned
it off (use `--follow=false` to turn it off). In a terminal, the end of the input (Ctrl+D) stops a command that watches
or follows, like Enter does.

=== camel-jbang - camel cmd route-diagram and camel cmd span

* `camel cmd route-diagram --highlight` with node ids that no route has exits with `3` (it drew the diagram with
  nothing highlighted). Its errors are printed on stderr, and a graphical `--theme` in a terminal without graphics exits
  with `2`.
* `camel cmd span --trace` with a trace id that matches no trace exits with `3` (it printed a message and exited with
  `0`), and an unknown `--sort` key of the traces exits with `2` (it sorted the newest first).

=== camel-jbang - camel run: startup in the background, and input errors

* `camel run --background` waits up to 60 seconds for the integration to start (it was 20 seconds), and
  `--background-wait` takes how long, such as `--background-wait=2m` (`true` and `false` still work). An integration
  that is still starting after the wait exits with `124` and is left running; before, the command exited with `0`.
* The output of an integration in the background goes to `~/.camel/<pid>-run.log`. The log is
  kept while the integration runs (it was removed once the integration had started), and `camel stop` removes it. With
  `--background-wait=false` the output goes to the log as well, instead of a pipe that nobody read.
* The output only gets the warnings and errors of the logging, and what the integration prints otherwise (such as why
  it cannot start): all of the logging is in `~/.camel/<pid>.log`, which `camel log` reads. Before, each line was written
  to both files. The `Log:` line is printed once the integration has started, with the path of `~/.camel/<pid>.log`.
* An integration that loads no routes exits with `1` and `No routes found in: <files>`, instead of running without
  doing anything, except with `--source-dir`, `--empty` or in dev mode, where files can be added later.
* `camel run` and `camel dev` with `--max-seconds`, `--max-messages` or `--max-idle-seconds` exit at the limit also in
  dev mode, which stopped the routes and kept running. A limit from the configuration (`camel.main.durationMax...`)
  still only stops the routes in dev mode.
* A file that does not exist exits with `66` and `File does not exist: <file>` for every kind of file, before anything is
  printed. Files together with `--source-dir`, and `--background` with a runtime that does not support it, exit with
  `2`.
* Only a run of nothing but Citrus tests exits with `2` and says to run them with `camel test run`, before anything is
  printed: `camel run test/orders.citrus.it.yaml`, or `camel run test/` of a folder that holds only Citrus tests.
  Before, it ran an integration with no routes. Citrus tests given with routes, such as by `camel run *`, are skipped
  with a line each (`Skipping <file>: a Citrus test, not a route (run it with camel test run <file>)`), and the routes
  run. A directory or `--source-dir` with a `test` folder runs as before. The `camel_run` tool of the AI tools does the
  same: it starts nothing for Citrus tests only, and its answer lists the tests it skipped.

=== camel-jbang - camel explain and camel harden

* The LLM configuration, the progress and the prompts of `--show-prompt` are printed on stderr, so stdout has only the
  answer of the LLM (with a header per file), such as for `camel explain route.yaml > explanation.md`.
* An unknown `--format` exits with `2` (it was ignored).
* `camel harden --api-type=openai` prints the suggestions (it printed only the header): the OpenAI-compatible service
  answers in one go, also with `--stream`.
* An API key of eight characters or fewer is not shown in the configuration (the last four characters of a key were).

=== camel-jbang - the AI tools report failures as errors

* `camel ask` runs a `camel` command for the model with a timeout of 60 seconds, and gives it the exit code, stdout and
  stderr as JSON (it gave the output only, and the exit code only when there was no output). A command that runs until
  it is stopped is not run, and the model is told the form that returns, such as `camel run --background`.
* The MCP tools of `camel mcp` and `camel tui --mcp` answer a failure with `isError`, instead of a normal answer that
  says what went wrong: `camel_run` of an integration that stops while it starts (`status` is `failed`), a restart that
  fails (`camel_control`), an action the integration answers with an error or does not answer (such as stopping a route
  that does not exist), and `camel_runtime_ai_trace` when the integration does not answer. The text of the error has
  what the answer had, such as the output of the integration.
* `camel_control restart` in `camel tui --mcp` waits until the integration has started again, or failed to start (it
  answered at once), and `camel_control stop-routes` and `start-routes` answer with what was done instead of the
  answer of the integration.

=== camel-jbang - the errors of a streamed answer of an LLM

* When the streamed answer of an LLM fails, as with `camel explain`, `camel ask` or `camel overview --ai` against
  Ollama, the error is printed on stderr: it was printed on stdout after the answer so far. A failure without a message
  of its own, such as a refused connection, has the message of its cause instead of `null`.
* `camel ask` with an OpenAI-compatible server that gives the arguments of a tool call as a JSON object, instead of JSON
  text, calls the tool with these arguments (it called it without arguments).

=== camel-jbang - camel ask: the exit code and the output of a question

* `camel ask` exits with `1` when the call of the LLM fails, such as with an error of the model or a refused connection:
  it exited with `0` without an answer.
* `camel ask` with a question prints only the answer on stdout. The model it uses, the integration it asks about, the
  note about a small model, and the output of `--show-tools` and `--show-stats` go to stderr.

=== camel-jbang - an LLM given with --url is the only one used

`camel ask`, `camel explain` and `camel overview --ai` fail when the LLM given with `--url` does not answer: they went
on to another provider, such as the Ollama of the host, or a hosted provider with an API key in the environment, and
sent the prompts there. The AI panel of the Camel TUI does the same with the URL of its settings. Without `--url` the
provider is detected as before.

=== camel-jbang - exit codes and errors of more commands

* `camel export` does not clean the export directory when it is the current directory, or one that contains it, unless
  `--clean-dir` is given: `--dir=./`, `--dir=$PWD` and `--dir=..` deleted the files of the project, as only `--dir=.`
  was recognized.
* `camel harden --url` with an LLM that cannot be reached exits with `1`, instead of sending the route to a local
  Ollama.
* `camel sbom` with an unknown `--sbom-format` or `--sbom-output-format` exits with `2` (it exited with `0` without
  doing anything), and exits with `124` when Maven does not finish in time.
* `camel update run --upgradeTimeout` applies (the output was read to the end before the wait), and exits with `124`
  when it is reached.
* `camel version list` exits with `1` when the versions cannot be downloaded. `camel version set --reset` removes the
  Kamelets version too, and `camel version get` shows a configuration with only the Kamelets version.
* `camel validate yaml` and `camel validate properties` with no file to validate exit with `66`.
* `camel doc` looks a name up in the catalog of Camel before it downloads the Kamelet catalog, so a component is
  documented also offline. `camel catalog kamelet` has `--repos`.
* `camel kubernetes logs` exits with `3` when no pod with the label shows up, and with `1` when reading the log fails.
  `camel kubernetes run` prints the end of the Maven output when the build or deployment fails (it was hidden without
  `--verbose`), and exits with `124` when the pod is not running after 10 minutes.
* `--jvm-args` keeps a quoted value with spaces as one argument.

=== camel-jbang - processes started by the CLI stop with it

* When `camel run` of a Spring Boot or Quarkus project (and `camel debug`) ends, such as on Ctrl+C, the processes
  started by Maven stop too: the app could outlive Maven, as it runs in a JVM of its own. A process that does not stop
  within 30 seconds is killed (it was left running).
* A plugin that cannot be loaded ends the command with exit code `1` and the error, as before, but no longer exits the
  JVM while the plugins load, which ended a program that embeds the CLI.

=== camel-jbang - camel overview and camel transform route

* `camel overview --format` other than `markdown` or `json` exits with `2` (it printed the markdown).
* `camel transform route --format` other than `xml`, `yaml` or `java` exits with `2` (it ran the routes and printed
  nothing), and a route that cannot be read, such as invalid YAML, exits with `1` and the error of the parser (it failed
  with an internal error, `70`, and a stack trace).

=== camel-xml-io, camel-yaml-io, camel-java-io - route dumps keep options set to their default value

The XML, YAML and Java route dumps (`camel transform route`, `camel validate normalize`, `camel.main.dumpRoutes`, the
developer console) now write these options whenever they are set, also with their documented default value: the
binding options of the rest configuration, a rest and its verbs (such as `bindingMode="off"`), the options of a
redelivery policy, of a `resilience4jConfiguration` and of a `faultToleranceConfiguration`, `shutdownRoute` /
`shutdownRunningTask` of a route, and `apiDocs` of a rest verb. Left out, such an option took the value of the parent
level when the dump was loaded (the rest configuration or the rest, the error handler, the global circuit breaker
configuration, the CamelContext), so the route behaved differently, or, for `apiDocs`, a verb with `apiDocs="true"`
under a rest with `apiDocs="false"` was left out of the OpenAPI document.

=== camel-yaml-dsl - a value under parameters: with an & is kept whole

In the YAML DSL, a value under `parameters:` that has an `&` is now kept whole in the endpoint uri. Before, the value
was cut at the `&`, and the rest was read as another option. For example, with
`+filterFile: "${file:name} contains 'R&D'"+` the route failed to start. A lenient component, such as a Kamelet, got
only the text before the `&`, without an error.

Camel now puts such a value in `RAW(...)` in the uri, as it does for a secret option. When the value has `)&`, Camel
uses `RAW{...}`. The value is then taken as it is written, so a `+` in it stays a `+`. A value without an `&` is read
as before, so a `+` in it is a space (such as in a cron expression written with `+`).

This applies to the endpoints of `from`, `to`, `toD`, `wireTap`, `poll` and `switch`, and to the `kamelet` EIP. Camel
does not change a value that starts with `#` (a bean reference), a value in `RAW(...)` or `RAW{...}` already, or a path
